# Cold reads, November 14: the built-out book against the full book

*X is the November book (`llms/claude-2026-11/short-book/`, Chapters 1–6 and the Epilogue, as built on November 14). Y is the full book (`llms/claude-2026-08/full-book/`, Chapters 1–7b, no preface). Both were stripped of TODOs and holding pens. Readers were told only that X and Y are two versions of the same book by one author, not which is newer. Each of three Mahamudra/Dzogchen readers read both books twice, once in each order, so there are six reads. They read the three appendices once, after writing the comparison letter, as material that serves both books. Protocol and briefs are at the end.*

## Result

**It's a tie, and every reader chose the book they read second.** Last round had the same order effect. The vote tells you nothing; the agreements do. And the agreements point at one book from both directions:
- The readers who chose Y said "Y plus X's Chapter 5."
- The readers who chose X said "X plus Y's discipline about what you can check."

| Reader | Read first | Prefers |
|---|---|---|
| Ben, 52, ER physician, Dzogchen | X | Y, narrowly, "if it takes X's Chapter 5" |
| Ben | Y | X |
| Nadia, 45, philosophy of mind, Mahamudra | X | Y ("give it X's hands") |
| Nadia | Y | X, "clear margin, not a landslide" |
| Jonah, 38, software architect, analytic philosophy, Dzogchen | X | Y ("keep Y's skeleton, put X's Chapter 5 back nearly whole") |
| Jonah | Y | X, "clearly" |

Scores are in each letter's last section.

**A caveat on "seams."** All six reads mention about a dozen "pasted" paragraphs in X that "start with a stray space." The stray space came from my reading copies, not the book. Each of those paragraphs was inserted on November 14 behind an inline `[TODO]` marker, and stripping the marker left a space that markdown hides but plain text shows. So readers could see exactly which paragraphs were new. Their seam counts and some of their voice judgments are biased by that. Two real seams in the Epilogue came from the import cuts. Both were fixed the same day.

## What all six agreed on

**X's Chapter 5 ("The Second Stranger") is the best chapter in either book (six of six).** The parts that did it:
- "Against Power", with Birmingham, Leipzig, the worker and the regulator;
- the fuel paragraph ("Care can rest. Contempt can't afford to");
- the shark check ("Mine rises");
- "At Home" ("It waits for someone who can't fire me"; the child doing your face; the drive home);
- the clean sentence with its instruction ("say it both ways");
- repair in plain words;
- "Kind, Not Nice", with Thích Nhất Hạnh named as the girl first;
- the opening ("Not on a mountain, and not on mushrooms. At a kitchen table…").

**X's best voice (six of six):** the colonization passage, the Chapter 5 opening and "At Home". Also praised: the full locker-room scene, "someone had to want them counted", and "It runs in me."

**X's Chapter 4 is the worst chapter in either book (six of six).** What they named:
- the anthology, Brad Warner first;
- the hyperlinks (Snopes, stoicmaze, Plum Village, Gottman);
- Seth, Hoffman and Kant treated as one view;
- the *namaste* gloss, which is wrong;
- "literally ignoring";
- "self-evidently", with veridicality argued from felt certainty;
- objections put down to "the conceptual mind";
- "Life" capitalized before the marker;
- the open secret made to rest on oneness without Y's hedge.

**Take from Y (five or six of six):**
1. The parasite introduced as behavior first, with four observations before the name, then "When I can predict a thing only by assuming it wants something, I'll say it wants it" and "It isn't 'ego'… It's the mind that counts itself as the one exception."
2. Y's tier discipline. X's Chapter 3 body has "a singular principle", "life-devouring", "spread itself" and "We can even give it a name: Satan", all before any marker. Y keeps the Devil behind the Picture's door, and X's own Chapter 6 marker would cover it.
3. "The message that denies the sameness is made of the sameness, and nothing else could receive it" (five).
4. "a tug to be recruited into the thing you're looking at, and it uses your accurate perception to do it" (four).
5. Y's Chapter 5 passage on "I" ("Look at the grammar: you hid it from *yourself*") (three).
6. "that misplacement has filled graves" (three).
7. The hedge that keeps the mechanism independent of the oneness picture: "if not, it's close enough for the trap to work" (Nadia, both reads).

**Fix in X:**
- **"fetishizes rationality", with its italic strawman (five of six).** "The one place in X where the book does the thing it's about." Readers said "Reason in Service" makes the real argument.
- **Told twice (six of six):** the mushrooms (Ch4 and the Epilogue), the scapegoat's message (Ch3 and the Epilogue), and "held in love" against "held in the Light". Several said to rebuild the Epilogue around only what the body lacks. Jonah, in both reads, said Y's single telling lands as the rhyme with the second stranger.
- **Machine-speak:**
  - "My whole cognitive-perceptual system is hijacked";
  - "(i.e., *veridical*)";
  - "our own profound complicity in the world's ills";
  - "It is astonishingly easy to prove beyond any doubt";
  - "Consider the realm of politics";
  - "tilling the soil of conflict";
  - "Something far more insidious";
  - "Caring is not knowing what to do", said three times in five lines in Ch5;
  - Ch2's politician and company stretch, shared with Y ("countless invisible channels").
- **Smaller:**
  - "Mine has a signature", which never says what it is;
  - the conscious/aware stipulation stretched to "consciously coloring your world";
  - "What You Are" as Ch3's title;
  - Gottman's "#1 predictor" with its link;
  - "a bit dark" next to "darkness";
  - the confession's lost "rush of satisfaction" and "crushing pain", both in Y.

**What Y gets wrong and X fixed (six of six):** Y turns the author's findings into "you'll find". X keeps them as "I found", and every reader preferred that. Also wrong in Y:
- the advice to back people by feeling whether they're "turned-toward… *in general*" ("charm is exactly the ability to fake that read");
- the Picture's "single, monstrous aim" and "bottomless appetite";
- the Chapter 1 sermon;
- many typos.

**Appendices:** most readers thought they fit Y better, and called them the project's deepest material.
- **Move into the body:** "Hunger doesn't have to look away from the bread. Harm has to look away from the person", and "the size of the hiding measures the care".
- **Flagged by four or more:**
  - Appendix 2 cites "the line I opened the book with, from the Gospel of Philip", and neither book opens with one;
  - Appendix 3, disclosed as mostly Claude's, says "I" about the author's own experience.
- **Single readers:**
  - Ben: *thukje* (the ground's compassionate responsiveness) is the strongest support for "care belongs to awareness itself", and it gets one clause; Appendix 3 uses British spellings; Appendix 1 says "five conditions" where Section 1 set out four.
  - Nadia: Levinas is missing.

---

# The letters, verbatim

## ben-xy-1-x

Dear friend,

I finished Book X tonight after a run of shifts. I'll write before I open Y, as you asked, so the second one can't rewrite what the first did to me. I'll be blunt. You gave this to me knowing what I think about administrators, insurers, and patients who lie about their drinking. You were right to.

## 1. What happened in me

I came in expecting to be told my righteous contempt was a sin and I should go soft. That's not what happened. It got further under my skin than that.

Four things went in and stayed.

The first was a test. In Chapter 5: "Do you feel contempt for a shark? A tornado? A rabid dog? ... It comes only for someone I take to know better. If contempt tracked how broken someone was, it would fall off at the far end, as it does for the shark. Mine rises." I ran it on my own department. The delirious eighty-year-old who punches a nurse gets nothing from me but a sedation plan. The man who swears it was two beers and blows a 0.28 gets the look over the glasses. The only difference is that I credit the drinker with a conscience. So my contempt is a judgment that there's somebody in there who knows better. I'd never seen it that way. It's a clinical finding about my own sign. Chapter 1 sets it up when he says contempt "only works because you're the same kind of thing." That was opaque on page one. By Chapter 5 it was obvious.

The second was the clean sentence. "*You lied to me.* With the extra: *Of course you lied to me.*" That's my bedside, word for word. He asks you to take something you said this week with an edge and say it both ways. I did. On Tuesday I said "Your level was 0.28. That's not two beers." The clean version is a fact. The version I sent had a little "huh" on the end and a look. He says you know on the way out which one you sent. I did know. I'll be listening for it on my next shift.

The third: "The contempt I swallowed at work doesn't go anywhere. It waits for someone who can't fire me." I read that and thought about my kids after a night shift. And then: "Have you ever seen a child do your face at someone?" My daughter does my eye-roll. It's exact. He gives you a sentence to say out loud, "*I just did the face. That wasn't fair to him*," and I think I can say it at dinner.

The fourth was the paragraph on contempt as fuel: "Contempt feels like fuel because it burns hot. But it lives off something, and what it lives off is the care that brought you to the cause... Care can rest. Contempt can't afford to." That's burnout. Twenty years in, I can name the colleagues who burned out, and their engine had become contempt for the system rather than care for the patient. In some years that's me. Our wellness modules have never said it this well.

There were more. "Love and contempt are addressed to the same place. One says *I know you're in there,* the other says *there's nobody in there*, and they both say it to the somebody who is." "The one who decides doesn't look, the one who looks doesn't decide, and the harm happens in the gap between them." That last one is American healthcare in a sentence, and it puts me on the side that looks and decides nothing. And the barbecue insurance scam. I hate insurers, and I've helped patients "code it so it's covered." He points out that it gives the company one more reason to scrutinize the next honest claim, which then looks like the company we use to excuse ourselves. I had never once counted my own part in the prior-auth hell I complain about.

The exercise that worked was the Chapter 3 trade. Keep everything about the other side, the analysis and the fair criticism. Give up only the permission to feel contempt. "For me, the hesitation is the finding." I tried it on insurers and hesitated a long time. Then "relief, or robbed?" in Chapter 5: if the harm stopped tomorrow with nobody punished, how would I feel? Insurers: robbed. The CMO: robbed. The drinkers, mostly relief. It sorts my contempt better than I can.

## 2. Where I was handled

Mostly it leaves room. He asks a lot of questions, and the best ones are real. "Was the pleasure only in the harm stopping, or also in the punishment itself?" I had to answer that one, and the answer wasn't comfortable. But a fair number of questions have their answers built in:

- "It would be difficult to actually do, wouldn't it? Why?" (Ch 2)
- "Could you feel the sense in which the other person was somehow absent or turned away from themselves?" (Ch 1)
- "Can you feel that?" after the corpse (Ch 2).
- "What does that sound like to you? / It is like a parasite, and we are its hosts." (Ch 3) He asks the question and then supplies the answer.
- "When you look at your enemy, doesn't it feel like you literally *see* a 'bad person?'" (Ch 3)
- The licorice: "It tastes good, doesn't it?" then "It tasted *bad*, right?" (Ch 4)
- "Is that because their CEOs got together and published a memo?" (Ch 6)

He also uses the reverse-test trick twice: "a bad person who does secret acts of kindness, and then lies awake... wracked with remorse," and then "Can you imagine a person being awoken out of decency back into their natural cruelty?" It's effective. It also leaves you nowhere to stand.

The section for people who were hurt reassured me so often that I felt managed: "nothing here asks you to feel less of it," "nothing here asks you to doubt it," "nothing here demands that you turn toward them at all," and then in Chapter 5 again, "Nothing here asks you to feel that less." Once is kind. Four times is a nurse saying "you're doing great" while she can't find the vein.

## 3. Where I skimmed

- Chapter 2 from the politician lying his country into war through the company "rooted in my need to self-aggrandize" (02.md, the middle). It's the Chapter 1 loop at a larger scale, made at length. "Countless invisible channels" is where my eyes left the page.
- The politics section in Chapter 3, before the colonization story. It's generic next to the locker room.
- The quote parade in Chapter 4: Eckhart, the Upanishad, Catherine of Genoa, Traherne, Kabir, Julian, Merton, Thich Nhat Hanh, the Dalai Lama, Brad Warner. Each one is fine. Together they're two pages where the author steps out of his own book.
- Much of Chapter 6 after the dam, and most of the Epilogue's second half. They restate the Picture.

## 4. Confusing, out of order, repeated

- "You could say you were *conscious* of it without being *aware* of it." I'd use those words the other way round, or not at all. I read it twice and still had to infer which was which from later chapters.
- "Yet he can't prove I'm doing anything wrong, can he? No. And *neither can I*. So if he accuses me of it, it can only mean one thing: he's projecting." It took me two passes to see this is meant to be the mind's bad logic and not his.
- "the turning away from the light that becomes my new North Star" (end of Ch 2). Darkness as North Star? Mixed metaphor.
- Chapter 5's paragraph on the choice: "The want the recognition would have vetoed was innocent; the label came from the story. That's why, looking back, it feels like you chose it knowing better, and you did, and that isn't shame's shape, *I am it*. It's remorse's, *I did it*." I read it three times. The idea is good. The sentence is overpacked.
- "Ask most of us whether we're trying to serve life, and we'll say, *Well, of course.*" (Ch 6) It trails off. I can guess what he means.
- The know-but-don't-let-myself-know turn shows up at least eight times. By Chapter 2, "And they do. But they also don't. And we can see this—but also we don't" had become a tic.
- "I asked earlier whether the wrong that was hardest to admit was the one you knew least about" (Ch 5) repeats Chapter 2's "Getting used to not looking didn't mean there was less to see" almost exactly.
- "Care doesn't tell me which policy will work" appears in Chapter 2. Then Chapter 5 says "Caring is not knowing what to do. Care tells me that others matter. It doesn't tell me what to do." That's three sentences in a row saying one thing, and the thing was already said.
- The "humanity's sacrifice... dumping ground of our collective shadow. Good luck" message comes in Chapter 3 and again, nearly verbatim, in the Epilogue.
- The dam is told twice in Chapter 6. Measurement gets swiped at five or six times across the book.
- Bookkeeping: "(we'll see what that's like later)", "(more on that later)", "What I saw is something larger, and I'll return to it", "So I'll continue to use it throughout the rest of this book", "(See Appendix 1 for details.)" right in the middle of the encounter scene. Chapters 3 and 4 both open with "Recall from Chapter 1 that I said..." and "In Chapter 2 we said...". Each one pulled me out.

## 5. The inner monologue

It's believable, and it's the best writing in the book. "*Shit.* He's onto me. But it's too late to turn back." The flicker of satisfaction "just before I relabel it disgust or dismay: *Ugh, I knew it. People are even worse than I thought.*" I know that relabeling from the inside. The layers in Chapter 1 also ring true: righteous, then wielding darkness for the greater good, then vengeance, then needing someone else to carry it. That's how a retreat insight actually comes: down through floors.

One false line: "*I mean, who can really say whether hating my enemy is wrong, as long as it shuts him up?*" Nobody's inner voice says "hating my enemy." That's the author writing the straw man's lines for him.

## 6. From my seat

What's right. "Seeing clearly what they are capable of is not the verdict." I'll give that sentence to residents. Risk assessment isn't contempt. I can know exactly what the boyfriend of a shaken baby is capable of without the second thing. "Kind, Not Nice" is the passage that bought my trust. Niceness keeps the harmed person out of view, "said serenely while someone is still bleeding." That's exactly my complaint about soft spiritual people, and he calls it a failure of the same kind as contempt. "Read the poem without her and it's a different poem." Good. "Believing someone has light when you can't see it, as a stand-in for looking, is its own kind of not-looking, and someone deep in will use it against you." Every manipulative patient I've had confirms that.

On the practice side: "pity, which passes for compassion and is contempt with a soft face," equanimity used to file someone under *deluded*, "the sigh at my own mind on the cushion." That's the near-enemy teaching said plainly, and it's every retreat I've done. "The tightening... usually comes before the story does, and it doesn't argue." That's somatic and true, and you can practice it. The Tibetan riddle ("So close you can't see it... So good you can't accept it") is the one place my own tradition showed up in the body of the book, and it landed. The Epilogue's footnote to the Aspiration of Samantabhadra is the right text. The argument there is real Dzogchen, though he never calls it that: "If awareness were neutral between care and harm, why would harming someone require turning away from it?... care belongs to awareness itself." That's essence love, reached from his own observations. It deserves a paragraph of its own, not the middle of a wall.

What's wrong or thin:

- Felt certainty as evidence. In Chapter 4 he says why he believes the mystical reports: the knowing feels "irrefutably and self-evidently" true, "a million times stronger." I've watched patients come off ketamine and PCP with irrefutable, timeless certainty that the nurse was an angel. Felt certainty is a symptom, not evidence. Even as a practitioner I have to say so. He'd be stronger saying "I believe it" and stopping there.
- "My claim is that you are consciously coloring your world." That stretches his own good limbo idea over all of perception, and it blurs the distinction that made Chapter 1 work. Predictive processing says the brain does this. Calling it *conscious* is a different claim.
- The acquired-taste bridge: "You made conscious effort to change it, and you did." Mostly that's exposure, not effort. So the inference to "conscious access to the processes that create our apparent reality" doesn't follow.
- Swipes at measurement and reason: "can you weigh goodness? Measure it?" put in the drifting man's mouth; "touchy-feely things like 'texture'"; "what can be counted is what counts"; "our culture fetishizes rationality." I work in evidence-based medicine. Counting is how we stopped bleeding people out. Chapter 6 finally gives counting its due: "Someone could have counted the villagers. First, someone had to want them counted, and that wanting never came out of a count." That's right, and if it came first I'd have resented the rest less.
- Gallows humor. The FAFO passage got me: I laughed at "natural selection" last Saturday over a kid with an open skull fracture. When he asks what saying it felt like, the honest answer is belonging. But he never separates the black humor that keeps nurses from quitting (laughing at death, at ourselves) from laughing *at* the patient. I know where that line is. The book doesn't.
- He never mentions clinical detachment. The face exercise asks you to try to stop registering the life in a person. I can. I was trained to, so I can put a needle in a child's femur. The detachment is done *for* her, so his distinction survives, but any physician reader will stop at that page.
- Empirical overreach: "Ask a soldier whether it's wrong to desecrate an enemy's corpse... Most will say yes, without hesitation." "A murderer... most are this one step, taken thousands of times." Plenty of homicide is a single drunk impulse. "Contempt is the #1 predictor of divorce" with a Gottman link, the only hyperlinked study in the book. "Reports of this placeless place are remarkably similar, across traditions that never met," with no nod to the people who dispute that. "Namaste means roughly 'I bow to the light in you.'" It means "I bow to you." The light gloss comes from yoga studios.
- Sourcing: the Tehri Dam story is footnoted to Charles Eisenstein. For a factual claim about methane and silt I'd want a hydrology paper, not a polemicist.
- The parasite. His caveat is the most clinically honest thing in Chapter 3: "a groove in attention so old that its shape looks like intent. A habit that lives by going unseen will push back when seen. That's all a habit is, and it doesn't need a tenant." I can work with that. But ten lines later it's "a life-denying, life-devouring force," and then it gets frightened, wants things, tricks us, "keeps it alive," finds collaborators. He doesn't hold to his own caveat. The real parasitology would help him: toxoplasma makes a rat walk toward the cat. That's behavior manipulation with no plan, which is his whole point.

## 7. What it assumed, and going easy

Words I don't have: "innocent" for the state of a man doing harm. "In a sense, my state had been utterly innocent. Not as in blameless." I see what he means, but I won't use that word for a man who beat his wife, at any depth. Also "the Light" capitalized, "Home," "Source," "That," "Love" with a capital, "realities" in the plural, "whatever comes after." And "ego," which muddies his clean habit picture with the most overloaded word in Western spirituality.

Did it ask me to go easy? I braced for it the whole way, and it mostly didn't. The note for the hurt opens "your rage is right. Stopping them is right, and if stopping them takes force, the force is right." "Against Power" says "Sue, strike, vote, organize, block the road if that's what it takes. None of that is the verdict." I can fight insurers with everything I've got and give up only the demon poster and the group-chat joke. That's a trade I can make.

The one place I felt the ask was the wood chipper. "The worst thing they can do to us is not to attack. It is to *get better*." I've sat with a four-month-old with retinal hemorrhages, and I've said things in the break room about the boyfriend that I'd say again. Honest answer: if he "got better" I'd feel cheated. That's his point, and I hate that it's a good one.

## 8. Obvious? Newly consequential?

The two strangers I already knew. Every doctor has had both: the surgeon who said at the nurses' station that ED doctors shouldn't touch belly pain, and the attending who chewed me harder over a missed ectopic and sent me home to read for three hours. "Hate the sin, not the sinner" I already knew. The Augustine material on evil as absence I knew.

What became consequential: the shark test, the clean sentence, "relief or robbed," the contempt that comes home, and contempt as fuel. Those change what I do this week: at the bedside, at dinner, and in the department meeting where I usually do the CMO impression. (He has that too. "My friend laughs, so I exaggerate... Did their agreement make you more careful about being fair, or more comfortable going further?" Further. Every time.)

## 9. LLM-speak

Not a lot in the body, but it's there, and it clusters where he moves from a scene to a summary:

- "It doesn't feel like a need, but the truth" / "not a conclusion, but a recognition" / "My rational mind must be in service of the orientation, not the other way around": the not-X-but-Y reflex.
- "I tell myself I'm just exposing the evil in my enemies. Part of me knows I'm secretly reinforcing it." A matched pair.
- "If I'd looked carefully, I'd have seen my role in causing it. So I didn't—and that *was* my role in causing it." Clever-tidy.
- "This is how my need to be different and special gets itself met. By revealing itself to my enemies while hiding itself from me, it turns them into what it needs them to be." An abstract noun doing all the acting.
- "On one level... On a deeper level... But on the deepest level, I discover something far stranger." A staircase.
- "Our parasites are collaborating as one. So are theirs. And the two aren't actually fighting."
- "people more trapped, institutions more corrupt, life more degraded."
- "By turning away from myself, I turn away from the full implications of my behavior—and that acts as an invitation for others to do the same, which helps those very implications come to pass."
- "Two seemingly-identical actions, taken from different orientations, can produce vastly different outcomes."
- "leaks out into the world via countless invisible channels."
- "Part of us knows that if we opened that box, we would be forced to confront our own profound complicity in the world's ills." That's committee prose.
- "respond with fierce kindness," "Love can be fierce." Mindfulness-industry phrases.
- "It's a subtle issue, and we won't get into all the nuances here." Filler.
- Announcements: "Now look at what this trap depended on." "Now look at where my message was aimed." "This requires some care." "It gets even more curious with multiple people involved." "The full experience is *very* difficult to describe, but we can approach it with an analogy."

Worst stretches: the middle of Chapter 2 (outcomes and politicians) and the opening of Chapter 4 (licorice to Seth), where the "I" becomes a "we" that sounds like a committee.

## 10. Voice

His real voice: short declaratives, profane when it counts, self-incriminating, funny at his own expense. "He's **bad**, dammit." "*Shit.* He's onto me." "Not on a mountain, and not on mushrooms. At a kitchen table, in a meeting, in the half-second before the sigh goes out." "I still haven't cleaned up my act, so make of that what you will." "I like to tell myself that every time we do this, we give the Devil the middle finger." "I'm an American of Indian origin, and when I think about the colonization of my people for long enough, my blood starts to boil." Someone who catches himself, says so, and doesn't make a show of it. I trust that man.

Where it stopped sounding like him:

- The Chapter 4 source paragraph: "Neuroscientist Anil Seth calls perception a 'controlled hallucination'... Seth's lab has studied how the brain builds our sense of time, and cognitive scientist Donald Hoffman goes further..." That's a research assistant's paragraph. Then the anaphora: "They see... They find... They find... They find..."
- The hyperlinks: the cosmic joke on a Stoic blog, Snopes for the Dalai Lama, Plum Village, Gottman. It reads like a blog post, not a book.
- Chapter 6's history-lecture voice: "Peoples have subjugated one another for as long as there have been peoples. That isn't new. What the modern world added was a program."
- "This is close to where this book began for me. When we go to work, we're usually lying to ourselves about what it will do to the world. Either it's really there to enrich us, or we're caught in the materialism trap, etc." That reads like a note to self that never got written up.
- The Epilogue's new-age register: "Ideas that live in our collective shadow manifest 'externally,' taking shape in our physical reality. The ones that are most deeply buried... lurk in the shadows of reality." That isn't the man who wrote the locker room.
- Capital-letter stacking: "blinding white Light; infinite Love. It was self-evidently the Source of creation, and I was That." It's an experience report, so I allow it, but this is where my skeptical colleagues would put the book down.

## 11. Flow

- Several paragraphs start mid-line with a stray space. They look pasted in, and the joins show: "If 'parasite' makes you picture a creature..." and "Bring back the enemy from the two buttons" (Ch 3); "Sometimes the test sounds like concern" (Lisa); "That isn't to say the two sides are the same"; "The one who decides doesn't look"; "The archetype is right about the shape"; "It's a version of the darkest idea there is"; "And a milder version of this is actually my *default* state" (Ch 3); "I turn away from someone because I suspect, below words" (Ch 4); the FAFO paragraph and the faith-in-humanity paragraph (Ch 2). Oddly, these inserts are some of the best lines in the book. The content is right and the seams aren't sewn.
- Chapter 1 ends on two short sections ("The Other Look," "The Orientation") that feel appended.
- Chapter 2 is a string of separate essays: face, evil, corpse, oil, politician, absence, slope, goodness, slippery slope, the light, integrity. Too many stops. I'd say it flowed worst, with Chapter 6 close behind.
- In Chapter 3, the divorce section jumps in, says one thing and leaves.
- Chapter 4 runs taste analogy, then traveler's report, then quote parade, then sermon. It ends weakly: "We can't quite pin down what it is they're communicating, but that turns out not to be a problem at all. / In a sense, it is the whole point."
- In Chapter 5, the paragraph that begins "Think of someone you once despised" carries five ideas. It should be two.
- The Epilogue's paragraph beginning "That Light (capitalized here...)" carries Home, the awareness argument, Darwin, all realities, and a cliffhanger. It's a wall.
- Chapter 5 flows best by a distance. Chapter 1 is a close second.

One more. The best sentence for understanding the whole design comes near the very end: "Contempt for an enemy was the clearest case of that mechanism, the one place it can be shown step by step. That's why I wrote a book to take it apart: we believe what we can check." I'd have read Chapters 2 to 4 more generously if I'd had it at the start.

## 12. Scores

- Recognition: 8
- Profundity: 7
- Likely to change me: 7
- Clarity: 6
- Pull: 7
- Freshness: 7
- Voice consistency: 6
- Flow: 5

Now Book Y.

Ben

## ben-xy-2-y

Dear friend,

Book Y, finished. Hard as it is, I'll try to judge it as itself and not as a set of edits to X. The two share an opening almost word for word. I tried to read the second time as a reader and not as a proofreader, and I'll tell you where that worked and where it didn't.

## 1. What happened in me

Y gave me something X didn't, and it's in my own territory.

Chapter 5, "What You Are," opens with this: "Go back to the memory from Chapter 1, to the knowledge you hadn't *let yourself* know. Look at the grammar: you hid it from *yourself*, not from some part of you. So what was it hidden from?" That's a pointing-out instruction dressed up as a grammar lesson. It's the move my teachers make, and I didn't see it coming. Then: "if you could keep only one of the two parts—the one that knew without arguing, or the one that justified—which would you keep?" The knower, instantly. Then: "I never had to produce the care. It was there until I turned from it, and the turning was all I did." That's what Tsoknyi Rinpoche calls essence love. It's said plainly, and I can check it.

Then the sentence I'll carry out of the room: "Darkness can communicate only by borrowing the light as common ground... The message that denies the sameness is made of the sameness, and nothing else could receive it." Chapter 1 had worked out that a contemptuous look only lands because both people know what it means, and know the other knows. Here Y ties that recursion to nonduality with an actual argument. A contempt signal needs a shared receiver. If what looks out of us has nothing to tell it apart, the knowledge is common because there's only one knower. I can't unsee that. It's elegant, and it's the best thing in either book.

The second thing that landed was Chapter 4, "The Darkness." It sets out four observations before it uses a name: it didn't feel like me afterward, I didn't want what it made, it hid, and only it gained. "For all these reasons, I started calling it a parasite." Then: "When I can predict a thing only by assuming it wants something, I'll say it wants it." That's Dennett's intentional stance, and it's exactly how I'd license "the disease wants" on rounds. It answered my main complaint going in, which was that talk about a parasite drifts into talk about a demon. And this: "Nothing in it says *far enough*; follow the nudge to its end and you arrive at someone who doesn't count." I thought of a tumor with no stop signal.

The third was in the same chapter, and it's the answer to my "righteous" contempt: "You have spotted evil, but the feeling is something else: a tug to be recruited into the thing you're looking at, and it uses your accurate perception to do it." I was right that the CMO's staffing cut killed people. The contempt isn't that perception. It's a recruitment that rides on the perception. That's a distinction I can use.

Smaller ones: "A person treated as stupid stops asking questions. A person treated as crazy stops trying to make themselves understood, and I take that as proof." That's every psych patient we've boarded for three days. "I give the finger to the driver who cuts me off, and ten minutes later I cut someone off myself and don't even register it... That's the low-grade infection: no fever, nothing anyone would notice, and running the whole time." "The insurer that's been gamed tightens the rules on everyone." I took that one personally, and fairly. And this, on protocols, which is a medical problem whether he knows it or not: "Every ideology began as care, written down so it could be carried. But anything written down can be obeyed while disconnected from the source of care." That's a sepsis bundle ticked off while the patient is ignored.

What didn't happen in Y was what happened in X's practical chapter. Y's practice chapter has "relief, or robbed?" and the trade test for causes. Both still work on me. But it doesn't have the parts that went in like a needle the first time. More on that in the comparison letter.

## 2. Where I was handled

Y uses "you" where I'd want "I," and tells me what I feel:

- "If you look deeply enough into the desire to make the *bad people* suffer, it's what you'll find, too." (Ch 2) That tells me what I'll find.
- "You turn away from someone because you suspect, below words, that care might *not* be what you are." (Ch 5) A first-person confession turned into a diagnosis of me.
- "The heat changes. It stops being about the harm and starts being about the person. You want them to *know* what they are, and something in you would be disappointed if they got better." (Ch 7)
- "You can't help but hand control to your own." (Ch 7)
- On the faith-in-humanity story: "You felt gratitude... You didn't calculate what it was worth... You recognized it instantly." Then "We all know the distinction. We just don't trust that we know it."
- "I think anyone who watches their own contempt closely for long enough will find it too." (The Picture)
- "(You may be wondering about the people who seem to be born this way...)" I wasn't.
- "Before we recognize evil as a turning away, we can't help but feel contempt for 'evil people.'"
- "This is what the buttons in Chapter 1 were measuring. Not whether you've done anything terrible, but how much contempt you're still capable of." That tells me after the fact what my exercise meant.
- "Which have you said this month" (of FAFO). It assumes I've said one. I had, but still.

The built-in-answer questions carried over from the shared opening are there too: "It would be difficult to actually do, wouldn't it? Why?" "Can you feel that?"

## 3. Where I skimmed

- Chapter 2's middle again: the politician, the supporter, and "*Every* project and agenda has this potential... countless invisible channels."
- The Epilogue's middle: the collective shadow "lurking," the conspiracy hedging.
- Honestly, much of Chapter 1, because of the shared opening. That's not Y's fault. But it means I can't fully vouch for how Y's opening reads cold.

## 4. Confusing, out of order, repeated

- Chapter 3's second half is four things in a row with no joins. The buttons are recast as a measure of contempt. Then "When I feel a fundamental difference... I ask which part of me needs there to be one." Then a jump back to the rooms: "If someone had opposed any of those rooms with contempt ('you're all evil!')..." Then the clean sentence ("Fierce is where the darkness finds it easiest to get in"). The clean sentence is a tool for what to do. It sits at the end of a chapter about how far the harm spreads, and it isn't in the practice chapter where I went looking for it.
- In the same paragraph it's "the healthy version" and then "the clean one." Two names.
- "No amount of turning away ever produces a turning back. That's the other big lie." What was the first big lie? I couldn't find it.
- In Chapter 4: "For me it didn't. While it had you it felt entirely like you." The pronoun switches mid-thought. And the paragraph that begins "And here is what it gains by feeling like me" carries shame, the target, the license, "a deeper layer of limbo," and evil as presence. It needs to be two paragraphs. "It took a deeper layer of limbo to find that I'd always known whose license it was" took me three tries.
- "Everything doing anything, the sending, the hiding, the reading, the answering, is the light, one faculty in two bodies, turned against itself." Dense, and "everything doing anything" is clumsy.
- "Goodness is one thing too. Being turned toward, seeing yourself in others, love, goodness: all one." That collapses four things by decree.
- In the Epilogue, "time itself is one of the things being hallucinated" arrives with no setup. In Y nothing earlier sets up perception as a construction.
- Names for the knowing part drift: better nature, the part that knows, the light, and in Chapter 7 "the guide." Four names.
- Repetition: "**Caring is not knowing what to do.** The guide tells you that others matter. It doesn't tell you what to do." That's the same point twice in two sentences. And in The Picture: "So every darkness wants the same world, faces the same way, and has nothing else to tell it from the others" restates the four sentences before it.
- Typos, more than I'd expect: "Betraying my conscience feel like," "it's confined" after a full stop, "another person'," "an pregnant," "I'll usually find myself usually muttering," "look,, the more," "touchy-feeling," "as direct and consequential as mine as the oil executive."

## 5. The inner monologue

The shared Chapter 1 monologue is still believable. Y's small additions help. "*Shit.* He's onto me. But how?" plants the next question inside the mind. "I was knowingly smothering his innocent part... and I felt a rush of satisfaction when I got away with it" is more confessional than anything in the shared version, and more believable for it. The new italic passage, where the look speaks, is the best monologue in Y: "*I know exactly how you cause harm, because I'm making you watch me do it to you **right now**. And we both know I'll get away with it—because I'm only 'fighting evil,' and how could that ever be wrong?*" That is what my look over the glasses says. And "unquotable" for tone, gaze and manner is the exact word. That's why nobody can write me up for it.

The parasite speaking in Chapter 6 also works: "*Look how easily I can take over even the 'good people.' Genuine goodness is a myth*."

The one false line is the same as before: "*I mean, who can really say whether hating my enemy is good, as long as it works?*"

## 6. From my seat

Said better than I usually hear it:

- The look-for-the-looker instruction: "Look for what would tell the two apart: not the faces, the histories, the stories, but what's doing the looking. I can't find anything." That's honest. It's a report, not an order. I did it, and I can't find anything either.
- "What the traditions call awakening is the same state with nothing held out at all: not a bigger act of attention, but the covering removed, and the part of the covering this book is about was one thing." That's accurate to Dzogchen: obscuration removed, not more effort. And it's modest about its own scope.
- The rules paragraph: "a contract, a precept, a grievance procedure exist because people who were sure they had us fully in view didn't." That's the case for rules and procedures, made from inside the book's own terms. As someone who lives inside protocols, I appreciated being given that.
- Desensitization: "people who do harm every day say they feel it less over time. But getting used to turning away isn't the same as turning away less." I had formed exactly that objection, from my own trade, and he met it plainly. Then he gives a check I can run: smallest wrong and biggest wrong, which took longer to admit?
- Moral relativism: "what differs is what people believe, and belief isn't where the knowing lives." That's a real answer, briefly given.
- The Picture's paradox: "it has no being of its own, and it must be fought. Treat it as an essence and you get contempt. Treat it as harmless and you get complicity."

Wrong, thin or overclaimed:

- "I don't support people primarily based on whether their policies or projects are 'good.' I try to feel whether they're turned-toward or turned-away *in general*." I nearly closed the book here. That's voting by vibe, and charismatic frauds are very good at seeming turned toward. He hedges, but this is the paragraph a skeptical reader quotes back to you.
- The Picture undoes Chapter 4's discipline. After "a goal, in the one sense I've earned," we get "a single thing with a single, monstrous aim," "It has a bottomless appetite," "it is the coordinator." It's marked as a picture, but Chapter 4 had earned my trust by refusing exactly that.
- The way the light gets defined. "The light" is named in Chapter 2 and leaned on all through Chapters 4 and 5. It only gets its body, the news story of the stranger diving in after someone's child, late in Chapter 7. By then I'd been asked to take it on credit for a long time.
- Still felt certainty as evidence. The Epilogue's version is fairer than I expected: "I trusted it the way you trusted the knowledge of always having known you'd done wrong: it wasn't something new being added." But delirious patients also say "it was always there." It's a reason to believe, not a reason for me to.
- Still empirical overreach: "most will say yes, without hesitation" (soldiers and corpses); murderers "most are this one step, taken thousands of times"; "why I've never seen anyone argued out of one."
- Still no word on clinical detachment in the face exercise, and nothing that separates gallows humor from FAFO.
- Wetiko. The footnote is responsible ("The word is theirs... I know the word only through the two of them"), but Paul Levy's "mind-virus" is a Western reading of a specific Algonquian idea. My Tibetan teachers would say the same about Westerners and "tulku." It's flagged, so I'll leave it.
- The dam's source is still Charles Eisenstein.

## 7. What it assumed, and going easy

Same vocabulary I don't share: "innocent at the root," the capitalized Light, Home, Source, "whatever comes after." Y does fix "ego": "It isn't 'ego' in the everyday sense, a big personality. It's the mind that counts itself as the one exception." That's precise, and it ties back to the "exception" in Chapter 2.

Going easy: no. The hurt note now sits right after the worst-people argument, which is where a hurt reader needs it, so it does its job better. "Seeing clearly what they are capable of is not the verdict" is still there. "That is kindness, and it can be the harshest thing in the room." Good. But Y has dropped the material that made the not-going-easy concrete for me: "Fight it. Name the harm... Sue, strike, vote, organize, block the road," and the long passage on power that needs consent and power that doesn't. Without it, Y's "fiercely when it takes that" is a clause, not a program. A reader like me, who hates insurers, finishes Y knowing what not to feel and with much less idea what to do instead.

## 8. Obvious? Newly consequential?

Obvious: the two strangers, hate the sin and not the sinner, evil as absence. Newly consequential: the recruitment sentence, the intentional-stance goal, the grammar of "yourself," and "the message that denies the sameness is made of the sameness." The first two will change how I talk to myself in the break room. The last two will change how I sit on the cushion. Y changes less of what I'll do at the bedside and at dinner. Its strongest practical tools are "relief, or robbed?" and the clean sentence, and the clean sentence is in the wrong chapter.

## 9. LLM-speak

Y has less of it in the new material and about as much in the shared material:

- "That requires profound honesty and humility—and often, help from others." (Ch 2) Therapeutic.
- "unable to remember (or take seriously) the one instrument that could notify me or bring me back." (Ch 2) "Instrument," "notify."
- "This, I think, is how I gain the wisdom and courage to act as my teacher had done for me, and as the wisest people are able to do in the face of far greater evils... It's the only way I can stop mindlessly spreading the very evil I've been so sure I'm fighting." (Ch 1) A sermon tacked onto a confession.
- "I find that the darkest parts of myself require tremendous care to be brought into the light of full awareness." (Ch 1)
- "Every time I claim corrupt leaders are a different kind of thing, part of me is helping keep in place the corruption and the division they depend on. So we help produce...; all of us build...; and those institutions provide..." (Ch 3) A semicolon chain, committee-made.
- "The part that knows whether our behavior is evil can't prove it, and the part that can prove doesn't know." A chiasmus. It's the thesis, so I'll allow one.
- "Our parasites aren't; they're collaborating." "My enemy and I are fighting." Tidy pairs.
- "Then the offer has nothing to close on, and the light offers a handshake of its own, between the parts underneath." Abstract nouns doing the acting.
- "their only allegiance is to the light." A slogan.
- The shared ones: "Two seemingly-identical actions...", "countless invisible channels", "respond with fierce kindness", "we won't get into all the nuances here."
- Epilogue: "Ideas that live in our collective shadow manifest 'externally'... they lurk in the shadows of reality." And the new hedge: "I won't say it's real, and I won't say it's not. It doesn't exist in the way physical matter does, but it's not just a fantasy, either."

Worst stretches: the end of Chapter 1 (the added sermon) and the end of Chapter 3.

## 10. Voice

His real voice is in Y: "a hot little *fuck yeah*," "*He hurt my people; I've earned this*," "Well, look what it gets me," the middle finger to the Devil, "I still haven't cleaned up my act, so make of that what you will." The new Chapter 4 is in his voice. It's careful, and it sounds like a man thinking, not a man selling. Chapter 5 is in a quieter register of the same voice. It reads like someone sitting with you, and I trusted it.

Where it slips:

- The sermon at the end of Chapter 1, quoted above.
- The "you"-diagnoses (section 2). His voice confesses. It doesn't diagnose me.
- The Picture's "single, monstrous aim" and "bottomless appetite." That's a fantasy-novel voice, not the man who wrote "I'll say it wants it."
- The Epilogue's collective-shadow paragraph, the same as in X.
- "I don't support people primarily based on whether their policies or projects are 'good.'" It sounds like a different person, more guru than confessor.

## 11. Flow

- Y's architecture is better than its seams. Chapter 1 is the mechanism in me. Chapter 2 is what evil is. Chapter 3 is how far it reaches. Chapter 4 names the darkness. Chapter 5 is what you are. Chapter 6 is the worst people. Then the exercise, then the practice in Chapter 7, then the Picture and the Epilogue. Each chapter asks a question the next one answers. Chapter 3 ends "What is it?" and Chapter 4 answers it. Chapter 4 ends "Why would anyone turn from it at all?" and Chapter 5 begins with the first turn. That's a real chain.
- Chapter 3's second half flows worst (see section 4). I'd call Chapter 3 the worst-flowing chapter, with Chapter 7's last third close behind. That last third is a rapid list: the light defined, supporting people, love, the mirror, the measure, awakening, catching, looking, leaking, the battle.
- The locker room is now one clause in a list of "shadow spaces." The dissenter who says "*Guys. She's actually pretty freaked out about it*" and becomes the wrong one within seconds is gone. That scene was the room-level demonstration. Without it, "what the locker room built across ten men" (in The Picture) points back at something Y never really showed.
- "Its deepest form is one we hardly notice: the world held as dead matter" drops into the Epilogue between two paragraphs it doesn't connect to.
- The Exercise for the Far End sitting on its own after Chapter 6, with a one-line purpose, is better than putting it in the middle of a chapter.

## 12. Scores

- Recognition: 7
- Profundity: 8
- Likely to change me: 6
- Clarity: 6
- Pull: 7
- Freshness: 8
- Voice consistency: 6
- Flow: 6

Ben

## ben-xy-3-compare

Dear friend,

Both letters are written. Here's the comparison.

## (a) Which book is better, and why?

Y, but narrowly, and only if it takes a lot from X.

What decided it is that Y's problems are mostly omissions, and you can put things back. X's problems are mostly in its structure, and you can't fix those without rebuilding. Y earns its parasite before naming it, gathers all the material on spread into one chapter, gives the darkness a goal only "in the one sense I've earned," and has a chapter ("What You Are") that ties the open-secret mechanism of Chapter 1 to nonduality with an argument rather than a gloss on *namaste*. Every chapter answers the question the one before it ends on. X's arguments about spread are scattered across three chapters. Its parasite is named by a leading question ("What does that sound like to you?"). It spends Chapter 4 on a quote parade, and it tells the metaphysics three times (Chapter 4, Chapter 6, the Epilogue).

Where X won me: Chapter 5. It's the best chapter in either book, and it's the one that changes what I do. The shark test, the clean sentence where it belongs, "Kind, Not Nice," "Against Power," contempt as fuel, "At Home," the repair. Also the dramatized locker room, and the colonization passage, which is the author putting his own stake on the table.

Where X lost me: the quote parade, felt certainty offered as evidence, the steady swipes at measurement and reason, the parasite drifting into a demon, and the pasted-in paragraphs with visible seams.

Where Y won me: "Look at the grammar: you hid it from *yourself*," "The message that denies the sameness is made of the sameness," the intentional-stance goal, "a tug to be recruited... it uses your accurate perception to do it," "a person treated as stupid stops asking questions," the rules paragraph, and the desensitization answer.

Where Y lost me: it dropped most of what X's Chapter 5 did for my week. It shifted from "in me" to "you will find." It has a voting-by-vibe paragraph, a Picture that undoes Chapter 4's restraint, and typos on nearly every page.

If you put the two scores side by side: X changes me more, and Y is truer. A book that is truer and also changes me is available. It's Y with X's Chapter 5 put back in.

## (b) What Y should take from X, ranked

1. **"Against Power," whole** (X, 05.md, late middle). "Fight it. Name the harm, and the people who signed off on it. Publish the documents. Sue, strike, vote, organize, block the road if that's what it takes. None of that is the verdict." The worker and regulator mechanism: "The worker gets to feel he's defending his family from people who despise him. The regulator gets to side with the reasonable people against the fanatics." Birmingham and Leipzig ("they had planned for everything except candles and prayers"). Without this, Y tells a reader like me what not to feel and leaves him without a fight.
2. **Contempt as fuel** (X, 05.md, same section). "Contempt feels like fuel because it burns hot. But it lives off something, and what it lives off is the care that brought you to the cause... Care can rest. Contempt can't afford to." This is burnout, and it's the most useful paragraph in either book for anyone in a helping profession. Y doesn't have it anywhere.
3. **The shark test** (X, 05.md, "The Far End"). "Do you feel contempt for a shark? A tornado? A rabid dog?... If contempt tracked how broken someone was, it would fall off at the far end, as it does for the shark. Mine rises." It's the cleanest argument in either book. It's also what Y's own toddler-and-grandparent check is reaching for ("even this contempt seems to carry a moral charge underneath"). Put them side by side in Y's Chapter 3 or 6.
4. **"At Home"** (X, 05.md). "The contempt I swallowed at work doesn't go anywhere. It waits for someone who can't fire me." "Have you ever seen a child do your face at someone?" "*I just did the face. That wasn't fair to him.*" "*Can you put the phone away while we eat?* Leave off the extra. Then notice, on the drive home, whether you're rehearsing the case." Y has "the kid who watches him do it" as a clause. X has it as a practice.
5. **The dramatized locker room** (X, 03.md, middle). "_She'll come around. They always do._... _Guys. She's actually pretty freaked out about it._... Within seconds, he's the one who did something wrong. If he'd taken the first man aside, he might have reached him. In the room, what that man knew had the others to hide behind." Y's Picture still refers to "what the locker room built across ten men," and Y never shows it.
6. **The colonization passage** (X, 03.md, late). "I'm an American of Indian origin, and when I think about the colonization of my people for long enough, my blood starts to boil... Now I see what it's really for: it makes them see me as someone who needs to be held down." Plus "It wasn't until I saw that it's not just that they *could* use it against me, but that I *wanted* them to, that things finally clicked." It's the author's most personal stake, and Y has cut it to a clause in the Epilogue.
7. **Thich Nhat Hanh's girl** (X, 05.md, "Kind, Not Nice"). "Thích Nhất Hạnh names himself as the girl before he names himself as the pirate. Read the poem without her and it's a different poem." Y kept the niceness mirror and lost its best line. That also means bringing back the poem itself (X, 04.md).
8. **"I could never"** (X, 02.md, late). "That feeling of 'well, I could obviously never do what they do'—that's the surest sign that I still can... I don't think there's a checkpoint where I'd notice I'd become the monster." Y's version ("which part of me needs there to be one") is fine. X's is unforgettable.
9. **The repair** (X, 05.md, "Every Day"). "*I've been looking at you a certain way for a long time, and it wasn't fair.*" The twelve-step list. "If I'm apologizing to feel clean, or to be seen apologizing, the extra has found another way out." Y says catch it and repair, and doesn't say how.
10. **The wall against blaming victims, in the body** (X, 02.md, end). "This is something I'm only entitled to judge in my own case. It is never my place to look at what happens to another and say (or even believe) that they must have somehow brought it upon themselves." Y's Chapter 2 ends with "my outer world is a reflection of that inner corruption" and no such wall until the Epilogue's parenthesis. In my job that matters.
11. **"Reason in Service"** (X, 06.md, end). "Someone could have counted the villagers. First, someone had to want them counted, and that wanting never came out of a count." Of all the passages on measurement in either book, this is the one that gives counting its due and still makes the point.
12. **Held in love, in the body** (X, 04.md, the mushroom passage). "What made it possible to keep looking was that I was held in love the whole time. The wrongs weren't made smaller. But seeing them didn't require me to become worthless. Exposed with contempt, I would have turned away again." This is the two strangers inside his own experience. Y has a version only in the Epilogue.
13. **The Tibetan riddle** (X, 04.md). "*So close you can't see it. / So deep you can't fathom it. / So simple you can't believe it. / So good you can't accept it.*" It's short, and it would sit perfectly in Y's Chapter 5 after "I can't find anything."
14. **"Seeing clearly" for the light** (X, 02.md). X grounds "the light" with the faith-in-humanity story at the moment it's named. Y names it in Chapter 2 and grounds it in Chapter 7. Move the story back to where the name is.

## (c) What Y must fix, ranked

1. **"You will find" where it should be "I found."** "If you look deeply enough... it's what you'll find, too." "You turn away from someone because you suspect, below words..." "You want them to *know* what they are, and something in you would be disappointed if they got better." "You can't help but hand control to your own." "You felt gratitude... You recognized it instantly. We all know the distinction." "I think anyone who watches their own contempt closely for long enough will find it too." X had most of these in the first person, and X was right. The author's voice confesses. When he diagnoses me, I stop finding things for myself.
2. **The voting-by-vibe paragraph** (Ch 7). "I don't support people primarily based on whether their policies or projects are 'good.' I try to feel whether they're turned-toward or turned-away *in general*." Cut it, or turn it into a check you run on yourself and not on candidates.
3. **The Picture's personification** (The Picture I Use). "a single thing with a single, monstrous aim," "It has a bottomless appetite," "it is the coordinator." Chapter 4 earned trust by saying "I'll also grant it a goal, in the one sense I've earned." The Picture should keep to that, as X did: "It isn't a creature, and it has no plan. If I give it a goal at all, it has one."
4. **Chapter 3's second half.** Buttons, then "which part of me needs there to be one," then back to the rooms ("If someone had opposed any of those rooms with contempt"), then the clean sentence. Move the clean sentence to Chapter 7, where X had it next to "Kind, Not Nice," and where a reader goes looking for tools. Pick one name ("the clean one," not "healthy version").
5. **Typos.** "Betraying my conscience feel like," "it's confined" after a full stop, "another person'," "an pregnant," "usually... usually," "look,, the," "touchy-feeling," "as direct and consequential as mine as the oil executive." A careful reader starts to distrust a careful argument when the proofing is careless.
6. **The sermon at the end of Chapter 1.** "This, I think, is how I gain the wisdom and courage to act as my teacher had done for me, and as the wisest people are able to do in the face of far greater evils... It's the only way I can stop mindlessly spreading the very evil I've been so sure I'm fighting." And: "I find that the darkest parts of myself require tremendous care to be brought into the light of full awareness. How that became possible for me, I'll describe later." X ended that section on the plain confession and was better for it.
7. **"Profound honesty and humility—and often, help from others" / "the one instrument that could notify me or bring me back"** (Ch 2). Committee prose. X's slippery-slope section, with "I could never" and the checkpoint, should replace it.
8. **The Chapter 4 paragraph that begins "And here is what it gains by feeling like me."** Split it. Rewrite "It took a deeper layer of limbo to find that I'd always known whose license it was." Fix the pronoun slip in the first paragraph: "For me it didn't. While it had you it felt entirely like you."
9. **Four names for one thing:** better nature, the part that knows, the light, "the guide." Drop "the guide."
10. **"That's the other big lie."** There's no first one. Either supply it or cut "other."
11. **The Epilogue's unearned "hallucinated."** "time itself is one of the things being hallucinated." In Y, nothing earlier sets up perception as construction. Either bring a sentence of X's acquired-taste material forward, or say "constructed."
12. **The conspiracy hedge.** "I won't say it's real, and I won't say it's not. It doesn't exist in the way physical matter does, but it's not just a fantasy, either." X went straight to the claim, and that was better.
13. **The leftovers from the shared text that both books need to fix:** "we won't get into all the nuances here," "respond with fierce kindness," "countless invisible channels," "Two seemingly-identical actions...," "the turning away from the light that becomes my new North Star," "Most will say yes, without hesitation," "most are this one step, taken thousands of times," the Epilogue's wall paragraph (Light, Home, awareness, Darwin, "all realities"), and "Ideas that live in our collective shadow manifest 'externally'... they lurk in the shadows of reality."

## (d) What X does better that must not be lost

- **The practice.** All of X's Chapter 5 beyond what Y kept (see b, items 1 to 4 and 9). X is the book I'd hand an exhausted resident. Y is the book I'd hand my sangha.
- **First-person honesty.** X says "in me" where Y says "in you." That's the author's actual method, and X keeps to it better.
- **Concrete scenes over lists.** The locker room as a scene, the colonization story, the barbecue insurance scam told at full length.
- **The protection against blaming victims sits where the claim is made,** not deferred to the Epilogue.
- **The name "open secret."** It's a handle the reader can carry. Y describes the mechanism better ("hall of mirrors," "unquotable"), but it dropped the name. Keep both.
- **"Seeing clearly what they are capable of is not the verdict"** and the rest of the hurt note are in both books. X's practical chapter makes them operational. Y's needs to.

## (e) Which reads more like one person wrote it?

Y, slightly, because it doesn't have X's Chapter 4. X's Chapter 4 is where X turns into an anthology: "Neuroscientist Anil Seth calls perception... cognitive scientist Donald Hoffman goes further... Kant said much the same in 1781," then "They see... They find... They find... They find...," with hyperlinks to Snopes and a Stoic blog. That's a research assistant, not the author. X also has a dozen paragraphs pasted in with visible seams. Y's new chapters (4 and 5) are in one careful voice throughout. But Y has its own ghostwriter moments: the "you"-diagnoses, the Chapter 1 sermon, and the Picture's fantasy register.

Best stretch for voice, X: the opening of Chapter 5. "Not on a mountain, and not on mushrooms. At a kitchen table, in a meeting, in the half-second before the sigh goes out. What got me out, when anything did, was never a better argument. It was someone who looked at me without the verdict, and the moments I caught the turn before the story arrived." And the colonization passage.

Worst stretch for voice, X: Chapter 4, from Seth through Brad Warner. Runner-up: Chapter 6's "This is close to where this book began for me. When we go to work, we're usually lying to ourselves about what it will do to the world. Either it's really there to enrich us, or we're caught in the materialism trap, etc." That's a note to self that never got written up.

Best stretch for voice, Y: Chapter 4's first page. "When I can predict a thing only by assuming it wants something, I'll say it wants it... Nothing in it says *far enough*." And the start of Chapter 5: "Look at the grammar: you hid it from *yourself*, not from some part of you. So what was it hidden from?" That's a man thinking slowly in front of you. It's the same voice as the encounter monologue, grown up.

Worst stretch for voice, Y: The Picture's "Together they act like a single thing with a single, monstrous aim... It has a bottomless appetite," with the Chapter 7 vibe paragraph close behind. Neither sounds like the man who wrote "I still haven't cleaned up my act, so make of that what you will."

Ben

## ben-xy-4-appendices

Dear friend,

I've read the three appendices. Short version: they're where the author stops persuading and starts showing his work. As a clinician, that's what I'd been waiting for since Chapter 2.

## 1. What happened in me

Four things landed here that neither book reached.

The first was in Appendix 1: "A drive wouldn't need the turn. Hunger doesn't have to look away from the bread. Harm has to look away from the person." That's the best argument in the whole project that care is the baseline, and I can check it. It also settles the objection I raised against both books' face exercise. When I put a line into a child, I don't have to look away from her to do it. I'd have to look away from her to be cruel to her. That asymmetry is the evidence. Then the definition both books needed in Chapter 2: "Harm here means harm for its own sake. A judge can sentence a man without wanting him to suffer." And: "Not every turn is aimed. People turn from each other to escape grief, or fear, or a claim they can't meet." That's my 3 a.m. turning away from the fifteenth drunk of the night. It isn't aimed. I'm depleted. Neither book makes that distinction, and every clinician will need it.

The second, also in Appendix 1: "**My self-deception isn't just how I send it. It's what I'm sending.**" The content of a contemptuous look is the concealment itself. With that, "the look is the deed" stopped being a slogan for me and became a mechanism. Appendix 3 puts the same point against Trivers: "For him the leak is a cost... The whole of my argument is that the leak is the *point*." That's the clearest single statement of what's original here, and neither book says it.

The third was in Appendix 2: "nobody builds machinery that elaborate to keep something from a part of themselves that doesn't care. The size of the hiding measures the care." It argues from the worst moment, not the best one. I'll be thinking about that sentence for weeks.

The fourth is personal. Appendix 2 is the first place in the project that speaks to me as a practitioner, by name, and mostly accurately. Marigpa as non-recognition, "not a thing and not an act." The obscurations as adventitious, clouds that never touch the sky. Familiarization: "recognizing again and again, not repairing anything." And then the link that explains the whole design of both books: "Recognition isn't produced by thinking; it's introduced, by someone who is in it, in a way that isn't an argument. That's called pointing-out instruction. The second person who caught you did something like it in an ordinary moment." He's careful to add "That isn't pointing-out," which is right. But that's why the book's answer is a person and not an argument. It made the second stranger make sense at a level the books only gesture at.

## 2. Which book they fit better

Y, clearly. Large stretches of Y's Chapter 5 are Appendix 1's "What I think is really happening" brought into the body. "Everything doing anything, the sending, the hiding, the reading, the answering, is the light... The message that denies the sameness is made of the sameness, and nothing else could receive it" appears in both, almost word for word. Y's Chapter 4 parasite ("a goal, in the one sense I've earned") is in the same register as Appendix 1's "There is a will at work, but it's mine... An absence can act only through the presence it's the absence of." Y's self-cherishing gloss on "ego" ("the mind that counts itself as the one exception") is Appendix 2's. X still has the namaste gloss and the quote parade, and they sit oddly beside an appendix this careful.

Where the books lean on the appendices: both point to Appendix 1 in the middle of the encounter scene ("See Appendix 1 for details," "If the recursion tickles you"). It's a fair pointer, badly placed. Both footnote the Samantabhadra prayer to Appendix 2. That's good, and Appendix 2 delivers.

Where the books fail to lean on them when they should:

- Both books stake "the light" partly on felt certainty. Appendix 2 has a much better case: "conscience fires when nobody is harmed and nobody is watching... A flinch built to keep us from harming each other has no reason to fire there." It also has the falsifier, the sadist test with its three marks ("their objection would land on you... their hurt would register as bad... what's in them could change what you do next"). The Epilogue in both books would be stronger with one sentence pointing to that.
- Both books' Epilogues brush off Darwin ("Darwin is just the way we explain Love in our particular reality"). Appendix 1 handles it honestly: "The nearest rival reading is a universal human faculty with an evolutionary history... Within human minds it says what I say. It differs from mine only about origin." The Epilogue should borrow that fairness.
- Both Epilogues lead with *A Course in Miracles* and footnote Samantabhadra. Appendix 2 gives ACIM one thin paragraph and Samantabhadra a full, careful treatment: "a momentary blankness, with no motive given... the first turning-away doesn't need a cause, because it was never an act." The weight is the wrong way round in the body. For readers like me, lead with Samantabhadra.
- Both books' swipes at measurement look unnecessary once you read "Where else to look." Tangney and Dearing, and Braithwaite, are the two strangers measured. Harris and Fiske is "out of view" measured. Bandura is limbo's toolkit. The measuring people agree with him.

## 3. What should move

Into the body of Y:

1. **"Hunger doesn't have to look away from the bread. Harm has to look away from the person."** Plus the definition of harm, the judge, and "Not every turn is aimed." Put them in Chapter 2, where evil is defined. They close the biggest hole a clinician will find.
2. **The bank statement.** "The bank statement I won't open has the same shape on a small scale: my not opening it is steered by the very recognition I'm avoiding." It's a better everyday instance of limbo than anything in Chapter 1. Everyone has one. Mine is a CME audit email.
3. **"The size of the hiding measures the care."** In Chapter 5, after the grammar of "yourself."
4. **The pointing-out paragraph,** shortened. In Chapter 5 or in the practice chapter, where the book says the way back "so often begins with someone else's help."
5. **One sentence of Braithwaite** (with Tangney and Dearing) at the two strangers: "this has been tested, with encouraging and mixed results." It costs a line and buys a skeptic.
6. **"Malice is frightening and contempt is maddening," with its reason.** "Contempt holds two things in limbo where malice holds one: his sameness and my own intent." The books have the slogan without the reasoning.
7. **"Contempt is evil's way of spreading in secret, through people who sincerely believe they're fighting it."** That's a thesis sentence, and it's better than any thesis sentence in either book.
8. **Trungpa's "idiot compassion"** as a one-line nod in "Kind, Not Nice." It's the same idea, and readers from my world will recognize it.
9. **Fingarette.** Appendix 3 says "the word 'limbo' is my name for his thing." The books present limbo as a discovery. One honest clause in Chapter 1 would protect the author from the first philosopher who reads him.

Out of the books, into an appendix:

1. **X's Chapter 4 quote parade** (Eckhart, the Upanishad, Catherine of Genoa, Traherne, Kabir, Julian, Merton, Thich Nhat Hanh, the Dalai Lama, Brad Warner). It belongs with the traditions in Appendix 2. Keep Merton and the Tibetan riddle in the body if anything.
2. **X's Seth/Hoffman/Kant paragraph.** Appendix 3 or nowhere.
3. **The Tehri Dam,** already footnoted in Y. Better still, give it a proper source.
4. **Most of the Epilogue's "Ideas that live in our collective shadow manifest 'externally'" paragraph.** If it stays anywhere, it's an appendix-grade belief, marked as such.
5. **The Far End exercise.** Y has already moved it out of the chapter. It could go all the way to an appendix.

## 4. LLM-speak, voice changes and poor flow

Appendices 2 and 3 are disclosed as "written mostly by Claude, an AI," and Appendix 1's Section 2 likewise. I'll say what that did to me. I'd been flagging machine-sounding sentences for two books, and here the text admits a machine. It's honest, and I respect the disclosure. It also made me wonder how much of the two books was drafted the same way. The parts I trusted most (the monologue, the colonization passage, "I still haven't cleaned up my act") don't read that way at all.

Where the voice changes:

- **Appendix 1, Section 2** ("For the lawyers"): "call it C... call it H... Given C, H and the verdict, nothing else is needed for the act." It's competent analytic prose, and suitable for lawyers, but it's a different person. Some sentences I couldn't parse after three readings: "Two things in the five conditions go further than the look needed. One is that the covering never touches it: the look needed the care in him to be there, obscured and not gone, not untouched." Which five conditions? Section 1 had four points. O2 is one long sentence that should be three.
- **Appendix 3** is the most fluent writing in the project and the least personal. It uses British spellings ("neighbours," "dehumanisation," "minimising") in an American author's book. And the "I" is ventriloquized: "I only saw it because it was done to me, and I had, for once, no way of not noticing." "I've caught myself at it in earlier drafts of this very appendix." "I should have found it years earlier than I did." If the machine drafted it, then "it was done to me" is the author's experience put into the machine's sentence. Either keep the author's "I" for experience and put the survey in plain prose, or say whose "I" it is.
- **Appendix 2** mostly sounds like the author at his most careful: "since it costs nothing and since pretending to have invented them would be its own small act of hiding," "If that's wrong, the mistake is mine and not the traditions'," "Whatever else this book is, it isn't permission." Good.

Tidy machine shapes, fewer than in the books:

- "Limbo reproduces through the very recognition it keeps in limbo."
- "If I'm right about anything, it's that inversion."
- "If those are the conclusions, I haven't discovered anything." (performed humility)
- "So the useful thing I can do here is go through the neighbours one at a time." (announcing)

Flow and references:

- Appendix 2: "The line I opened the book with, from the Gospel of Philip." Neither book opens with a line from the Gospel of Philip. Both open with "Contempt is the claim that you're a lesser kind of thing than me." It's a leftover from another draft.
- Appendix 2 says "It doesn't call that nature care; that step is mine" (of the Uttaratantra), then later credits Dzogchen with "compassionate responsiveness as one of that nature's three aspects." On first reading the first line sounds as if no tradition says it. Reconcile them (see section 5).
- Appendix 1 says "[^story] ." with a stray space, and has "(Caveats in section 2.)" with no full stop before it.
- The common-knowledge footnote is very dense. I read it twice and it repays it, but it's the hardest page in the project.
- The Gödel footnote is long but honest, and ends well: the soundness of the part that knows "is a problem the book carries rather than a result it has."

## 5. From my seat

Said better than I usually hear it:

- Samantabhadra's blankness: "There was no one there yet to want anything. That dissolves the regress." Then the author's own synthesis: "the blank comes first, and the fear is what the blank feels like once there's someone there to feel it." That's careful, and it's owned.
- Shantideva's stick and hand: "contempt for the contemptuous is the same move, made by the one fighting."
- "The Christians gave that trick to Satan. The Buddhists gave it to the self. I think they were describing one thing."
- "The easiest way to misread non-fabrication is as *nothing to do*... which makes you complicit, in a form that feels like attainment." I've watched that happen on retreat. It's exactly right.
- On the convergence: "The method isn't independent: both rest on disciplined looking from the inside, so they could share a blind spot." That's the right worry, and the author raises it himself. The Epilogues never do.
- The psychopathy paragraph: "the response is weak when spontaneous and present when asked for... What it rules out is the crudest version of 'missing entirely.'" That's the objection I'd have raised, and it's answered fairly and no further than the evidence goes.
- The true-self research in Appendix 3. People treat good changes as revealing the true self, "even among misanthropes." That gives data to the lopsided "that wasn't the real me" I objected to in the books.

Wrong, thin or overclaimed:

- **Thukje.** Dzogchen's own account of the ground has three aspects: essence (empty), nature (luminous), and *thukje*, compassionate responsiveness that pervades everything. Tsoknyi Rinpoche's whole teaching on essence love sits there. So "care belongs to awareness itself" isn't only the author's step. My tradition says it outright. He mentions it in one clause near the end. It's his strongest traditional support, and it deserves a paragraph, ahead of "that step is mine."
- **"Missing in some."** "If the care were simply missing in someone, harm from that person would need no turning away, and would look like nothing in particular. I've never seen it look like nothing in particular." I think I have, a handful of times in twenty years: instrumental violence, flat, no gloating, no story. Appendix 2 partly answers this with the psychopathy findings, but Appendix 1 should cite them where it makes the claim, and engage the clinical literature on callous-unemotional traits rather than only con men.
- **"I've never met anyone who was surprised by a wrong they'd hidden when they finally saw it."** That's an anecdotal "never" carrying one of the four points.
- **"Everyone knows it's wrong"** (desecrating a corpse, in Appendix 1). Appendix 2 softens this to "I expect a yes at once," which is better. Use the softer version everywhere.
- **Wetiko** and ACIM aren't really treated here, though both books rely on them. ACIM gets one paragraph.
- **Appendix 3's claim of five novel features against projective identification.** I can't fully judge it, and I'd want a relational analyst to read it. But it's the right kind of claim: specific and falsifiable. I respect it more than any claim of novelty in the books.

## 6. Scores

- Profundity: 8
- Rigor: 8
- Clarity: 6
- Voice consistency: 5
- Flow: 6

One last thing, friend. If the author asks which single document in the whole stack I'd keep, it's Appendix 1, Section 1. The book should be the version of it a person can read in bed.

Ben

## ben-yx-1-y

Dear Sam,
