- Chapter 4's anthology of mystics. The quotations are well chosen, but by Kabir I was skimming toward his own experience.
- The epilogue's second half, because a lot of it repeats Chapter 3 (see 4).

Chapter 2 is too long overall. It stops at fourteen stations.

## 4. Confusing, out of order, repeated

- "If I'd looked carefully, I'd have seen my role in causing it. So I didn't—and that *was* my role in causing it." Clever-sounding. On inspection, "my role in causing it" means two different things.
- "And what I've been calling *darkness* is exactly that self-deception: the turning away from the light that becomes my new North Star." I read it three times. What becomes the North Star, the light or the turning away?
- "Namely, if we really *saw* our fundamental equality with others, we would have to give something up... Not because anyone forces us to, but because something deeper in us wants to." Who wants to give it up, and if something deeper wants to, why do we resist?
- Names multiply in Chapter 1: "better nature," "the deepest part of me," "the you that knows you count," "his innocent part." Are these one thing?
- The parasite is "a groove in attention... it doesn't need a tenant" and then, four lines later, "a singular principle... a life-denying, life-devouring force." Then it's a trickster that is "tricking us into hating them to take attention off of *it*." Chapter 5 finally says it's "a stance, not a fact," which is where it belonged in Chapter 3.
- The chapter titled "What You Are" never gets to what you are. Chapter 4 does.
- Repeats: the inverse-remorse test (Ch. 1) and the "snapped out of being mean" test (Ch. 3) are one test. "The difficulty was a measure of how much I was keeping out" (Ch. 2) comes back in Ch. 5 ("I asked earlier whether..."). "Care doesn't tell me which policy will work" appears in Ch. 2 and again in Ch. 5, where the paragraph also says the same thing three times in a row: "Caring is not knowing what to do. Care tells me that others matter. It doesn't tell me what to do." Plausible deniability "builds itself" in institutions, Ch. 3 and again Ch. 5. "You will be humanity's sacrifice—the dumping ground of our collective shadow" appears in Ch. 3 and again in the epilogue. The evil-global-conspiracy material, including the clause "not to say that real conspiracies don't exist, or that we shouldn't fight them with everything we have," shows up almost word for word in Ch. 3 and the epilogue. The mushroom account appears in Ch. 4 and again in the epilogue.
- "This is why I said everything I do matters." I can't find where he said it.

## 5. The inner monologue

Mostly believable, and the best of it is very good. "he's **bad**, dammit." "If anything it's an *invitation*, to return to the realm of those who matter!" "it's the *good* kind of darkness—the kind that all good people must harbor, to fight the real kind!" I've said versions of all three to myself. The descent through layers (righteousness, then darkness for the greater good, then vengeance, then needing others to carry the darkness) is how self-inquiry actually goes. You hit a floor, then find there's another floor.

Where it fails, it's because a mind wouldn't say it: "*I mean, who can really say whether hating my enemy is wrong, as long as it shuts him up?*" Nobody calls it "hating my enemy" to themselves. That's the author putting the label inside the monologue.

## 6. From my seat

What he gets right, better than I usually hear it:

- Chapter 5's "Kind, Not Nice." "Niceness keeps the person who was harmed out of view: *it's all confusion, he couldn't help it*, said serenely while someone is still bleeding, because looking at them would mean acting, and acting costs." That's spiritual bypassing named more precisely than any Dharma talk I've heard. I've watched sanghas do exactly this about teachers who abused students. Also: "Believing someone has light when you can't see it, as a stand-in for looking, is its own kind of not-looking." And "Thích Nhất Hạnh names himself as the girl before he names himself as the pirate."
- Looking at the klesha itself: "look at the contempt itself, not the person. Where is it? What's it made of? In my experience it doesn't survive that, because there was nothing there to fight." That is Mahamudra instruction in plain English, and it's correct. "The tightening is worth learning, because it usually comes before the story does, and it doesn't argue." Good practical teaching.
- "Where the Choice Is." "Hold someone's eyes and decide, while holding them, to stop seeing them. See if you can find the moment." I tried it on my partner at dinner. I couldn't find the moment, and neither can any meditator; you only ever catch the distraction afterward. Then: "Nobody chooses evil under that name, and everyone is fully responsible, because responsibility never lived in a lucid instant of choosing evil." That's a real answer to an old puzzle. The villain/machine pair ("When I'm sure someone was fully aware... a villain. When I'm sure they had no idea... a machine.") is new to me and right.
- The naming trap: "*Goodness means serving life. This man isn't serving it. Therefore, hating him is good.*" The view hardened into a position. Madhyamaka in one line, without the word.
- Evil as privation. Augustine is used correctly, and "Evil doesn't have to originate from some kind of *bad essence*. It only requires a certain kind of confusion" is avidya, plainly put.

What's wrong, thin, or overclaimed:

- The nonduality is loose. "A single thing called *Life* is looking out from both our eyes." "Contempt is the refusal to see our equality, or our oneness." Equality and oneness are different claims. The book needs only the first. Sliding to the second costs him every skeptical reader and buys nothing the argument needs. "It is a turning from love, or God, or truth, or our better nature—whatever you want to call it." Those are four different things.
- "The Buddhists get to a similar place by another road... by rooting out self-deception, one layer at a time." No Buddhist school would summarize its path that way. And the Buddhists spent a thousand years arguing *against* exactly what the Upanishad and Catherine of Genoa ("My Me is God") are saying. Lumping them under "remarkably similar, across traditions that never met" is the perennialist flattening he then disclaims.
- "The Sanskrit word *namaste* means roughly 'I bow to the light in you.'" No. It's namaḥ + te: "bowing to you." "The light in you" is a yoga-studio gloss. Coming from an author who says he's of Indian origin, it's startling, and it cost him credibility on the whole paragraph.
- "*Ignorance*... we are literally ignoring it." The pun only works in English. Avidyā/marigpa is not-seeing, not ignoring.
- The Tibetan "riddle" isn't a riddle. It's the teaching on the four faults that keep us from recognizing mind's nature (too close, too deep, too easy, too excellent). I've taught those lines; I love them. "Beautiful riddle" is a tourist's phrase.
- The epilogue's "pure singularity: blinding white Light; infinite Love. It was self-evidently the Source of creation, and I was That." Every teacher I've had would call that a nyam, an experience of clarity and bliss, and say: don't take the experience for the nature; it comes and goes. Chapter 4 argues for veridicality from felt self-evidence ("irrefutably and self-evidently"), which is the one thing the contemplative traditions tell you not to trust. Two sentences earlier he says we're real "only conventionally," which is correct Madhyamaka. Then he reifies the ultimate as a Source, which is the move Nagarjuna spent a whole text dismantling. "This was what I was" and "the real me" swap one self for a better one.
- Yet the epilogue has the argument I most wish were in the body: "If awareness were neutral between care and harm, why would harming someone require turning away from it?... care belongs to awareness itself." That has real lineage. In Dzogchen the ground has three aspects, emptiness, luminosity and compassionate responsiveness. His footnote to the Samantabhadra prayer shows he knows it. That's the argument that would make the book cohere, and it's buried in a long paragraph that also covers Darwin and "all realities."
- Cognitive science: "consciously coloring your world, down to the level of qualities that you normally perceive as *intrinsic*" stretches "limbo" from motivated self-deception (where it works beautifully) to perceptual construction (where nothing is motivated). Seth, Hoffman and Kant are stacked as though they were one view; they aren't. "All three would call these processes unconscious": Kant wouldn't use that vocabulary at all. "Conscious vs aware" in Chapter 1 is a stipulation presented as ordinary usage, and it inverts how much of my field uses the pair.
- The "open secret" is good, and it's Lewis/Schelling common knowledge. But "If any of that weren't true, it wouldn't have the intended effect" claims every level of an infinite regress is necessary. Two levels would do.
- "One Darkness": "the dark in one is the same as the dark in the other." Lovely image, bad argument. Absences are individuated by what and where; two holes are two holes.
- "I didn't reason my way to this picture. I saw it first, and spent years afterward finding the reasons." Honest. But a reader trained by Chapter 1 will notice that "seeing first and then finding reasons" is the rationalizer's structure he diagnosed. He should face that on the page.
- Sources: a hyperlink to a blog for the "cosmic joke," a Snopes link for the Dalai Lama, a Gottman blog for "#1 predictor of divorce," and Charles Eisenstein as the source for the Tehri Dam methane claim. Each one drops the register and the trust.

## 7. Assumptions I don't share; going easy

The assumptions: that consciences all point one way; that there's a single Light; that "Life" is one thing. I can follow him on the first, as a stance I can test. The others I hold at arm's length, and he mostly lets me.

Going easy? No. Not once. This matters, so I'll be plain. "A note for people who were hurt" is wise: "your rage is right"; "The verdict is the one thing you don't owe them"; "Staying turned toward someone never means staying near them"; "It is possible to leave, cut contact, and never see them again with no verdict in it at all." "Fight it. Name the harm... Sue, strike, vote, organize, block the road." Birmingham and Leipzig, told well. Nothing here asked me to go soft on R. It asked me to correct the record without wanting him smaller, and I can tell those apart now.

## 8. Obvious? Newly consequential?

Much of it I knew: projection, bad faith, privation, the scapegoat. Practitioners know contempt is a klesha. What became newly consequential: (a) the should-know-better line, (b) the clean sentence. "*You lied to me.* With the extra: *Of course you lied to me.*" I said "Well, I assume you read the paper" to a colleague on Tuesday. Clean: "Did you get a chance to read it?" The difference is "assume," and I knew which one I sent. (c) "What I rehearse alone is what I see with afterward." That's my 2 a.m. R. rehearsal. (d) The child doing your face: "*I just did the face. That wasn't fair to him.*" My niece does my face. This week I'll say that sentence out loud at least once.

## 9. LLM-speak

Not much, honestly, and it's not the main problem. What I flagged:

- "This is how my need to be different and special gets itself met. By revealing itself to my enemies while hiding itself from me, it turns them into what it needs them to be while making me feel righteous for it." An abstract noun running a three-step program.
- "The corruption I produced in his mind: it's happening in mine, too." The colon-aphorism ending.
- "By turning away from myself, I turn away from the full implications of my behavior—and that acts as an invitation for others to do the same, which helps those very implications come to pass."
- "Two seemingly-identical actions, taken from different orientations, can produce vastly different outcomes."
- "imbue it with contempt"; "each helping the other ingratiate itself further to its host"; "using the situation to foster an ugly side of ourselves."
- "It's a subtle issue, and we won't get into all the nuances here." Committee hedge.
- "Their entire worldview—and thus apparent world—is internally consistent, and will vigorously fend off any attempt to puncture it."
- "My rational mind must be in service of the orientation, not the other way around."
- "Part of us knows that if we opened that box, we would be forced to confront our own profound complicity in the world's ills."
- "At their best, I think this is what the spiritual traditions are offering: a way out of that illusion." Back-cover blurb.
- The "On one level... On a deeper level... But on the deepest level" reveal in Chapter 3.
- "the worst thing they can do to us is not to attack. It is to *get better.*" Symmetry tic. Earned once; it recurs as a cadence ("The rage isn't the push; the verdict is"; "Care can rest. Contempt can't afford to"). The last two I'd keep.

Worst stretches: the middle of Chapter 2 (politician/company) and the opening of Chapter 3 (the parasite reveal).

## 10. Voice

His real voice, from the parts I trust: plain, first person, a little rueful, funny without trying. Italics for the narrated mind. Swears when the mind would ("*Shit.* He's onto me."). Goes first before asking you to ("I'll be honest: there is in me"). Concrete scenes over abstractions: the teacher who caught him stealing, the locker room, the coworker "really worried about Lisa." He's at his best saying a hard thing short: "the look is the deed"; "It waits for someone who can't fire me"; "I still haven't cleaned up my act, so make of that what you will"; "I'm on the lam from the Light."

Where he stops sounding like himself:

- The think-piece voice in Chapter 2 ("Consider..." / "*Every* project and agenda has this potential... countless invisible channels").
- The sermon voice: "It is that which turns us away from the light; that which usurps it." "our shared nemesis." "It is fought in your every thought, word, and deed."
- The pop-science explainer in Chapter 4: "Neuroscientist Anil Seth calls perception... cognitive scientist Donald Hoffman goes further... Kant said much the same in 1781." Plus the hyperlinks.
- The workshop voice: "So the most important thing we can bring to our work is our connection to the Light." And raw notes: "or we're caught in the materialism trap, etc."
- Greeting-card: "the light in me is reminding the light in him that it has a partner"; "I now think of them as its keepers."
- Chapter 5 is crisper and more aphoristic than Chapters 1–2. It feels like a later hand, still his, but a revised him.

## 11. Flow

- Strangest: a dozen single paragraphs that begin with a stray leading space and sit a little apart, as if pasted into older text. Ch. 2: " The audience has catchphrases, too..." and " Think of a news story that restored your faith in humanity..." Ch. 3: " If 'parasite' makes you picture a creature...," " Bring back the enemy from the two buttons...," " Sometimes the test sounds like concern...," " That isn't to say the two sides are the same...," " The one who decides doesn't look...," " The archetype is right about the shape...," " It's a version of the darkest idea there is...," " And a milder version of this is actually my *default* state." Ch. 4: " I turn away from someone because I suspect..." Epilogue: " It's still there in me now when I look." The irony is that these inserts are often the best sentences in their chapters ("The one who decides doesn't look, the one who looks doesn't decide"; the tinted glasses; "I suspect, below words, that goodness might *not* be what I am"). But the joins jolt.
- Ch. 1: the reassurance "if... you catch yourself thinking *I'm a terrible person*" is stapled to the end of the conscience section.
- Ch. 2: the bus paragraph raises a nuance and then says "we won't get into all the nuances here." The chapter keeps restarting at every "---."
- Ch. 3 flows worst. Parasite, the hurt, collaboration, locker room, politics, colonization, institutions, divorce, armageddon, elites, conspiracy, Satan, perception. Lines 163–181 cover five topics in eighteen lines. And the title doesn't match.
- Ch. 4 puts the claim before the analogy that would prepare it, and ends on a shrug: "In a sense, it is the whole point."
- Ch. 5 flows best. Each section answers the question the last raised. The callbacks pay off: "The arithmetic is the side that argues" answers Chapter 1's "Why did one side have to argue and the other not?"
- The epilogue loops back over Chapters 3 and 4.

## 12. Scores

- Recognition: 8
- Profundity: 7
- Likely to change me: 7
- Clarity: 6
- Pull: 7
- Freshness: 6
- Voice consistency: 6
- Flow: 5

Short version: Chapters 1 and 5 are a real book, and I'd hand them to my sitting group tomorrow. Chapter 4 is where a philosopher or a serious practitioner will put it down. The fix isn't less metaphysics. It's more care with it. He has the care in him; you can see it in "only conventionally so." He just doesn't keep it up.

Nadia

## nadia-xy-2-y

Dear Leila,

Book Y now. I read it the day after X, so the opening chapters landed on ground already turned over. I've tried to judge Y on its own and saved the head-to-head for the next letter, but I can't pretend I didn't read X first. Where the difference matters, I say so.

## 1. What happened in me

Y found the same sore spots and pressed one of them harder.

The contempt I'd rather not examine is mostly not for wrongdoers. It's for colleagues I think are stupid. Y says this outright, at the start of Chapter 3: "Little of my contempt is for the 'bad people' at all. It goes to people I've decided are stupid, or crazy, or weak, or slow: the coworker who doesn't get it the third time, the relative who believes something ridiculous. They've harmed nobody. The verdict is the same one, *this is all you are*, and the cover is better. With a wrongdoer I tell myself I'm fighting evil. Here I tell myself I'm only being accurate." Then it adds a line that cost me some sleep: "A person treated as stupid stops asking questions. A person treated as crazy stops trying to make themselves understood, and I take that as proof." I've watched a student stop asking questions in my seminar. I took it as confirmation.

Chapter 5 names what's under that: "You turn away from someone because you suspect, below words, that care might *not* be what you are... Under the suspicion is fear: fear of what you might be, which in public looks like pride." Fear that looks like pride in public is the most exact account of academic contempt I've read. My contempt for the "stupid" is fear that I'm not as smart as I need to be.

Then the passage I'd call the heart of the book: "Darkness can communicate only by borrowing the light as common ground... everything doing anything, the sending, the hiding, the reading, the answering, is the light, one faculty in two bodies, turned against itself. The message that denies the sameness is made of the sameness, and nothing else could receive it." That pays off the first sentence of Chapter 1, and it made the whole argument click for me as one thing.

And from my seat as a practitioner, this: "What the traditions call awakening is the same state with nothing held out at all: not a bigger act of attention, but the covering removed." That's right. It's the teaching on adventitious obscurations in one line, and I've heard Dharma teachers take forty minutes to say it less well.

The Last Trap (the beginner's question heard with a patient smile; equanimity as filing someone under *deluded*; "would I be glad if they got there before I did?") stung as much as before.

## 2. Where I was handled

- Y moves several of the author's first-person findings into the second person, and each time it tells me what's in me instead of saying what was in him. "If you look deeply enough into the desire to make the *bad people* suffer, it's what you'll find, too." "The heat changes... You want them to *know* what they are, and something in you would be disappointed if they got better." "You can't help but hand control to your own." "You turn away from someone because you suspect..." "A deeper part of you knows that in one case you've left the world a better place." Most of these are true of me. They'd still land better as his.
- "It would be difficult to actually do, wouldn't it? Why?" and "Can you feel that?" survive from X.
- "Would you have dug in? Vowed revenge? / The problem wasn't being caught." The answer still comes before I've given one.
- "(You may be wondering about the people who seem to be born this way...)" That answers an objection I hadn't formed.
- "I think anyone who watches their own contempt closely for long enough will find it too." A prediction about me, though hedged.

Where Y leaves room, it does it better than anything in X. "When I feel a fundamental difference between me and my worst enemy, I ask which part of me needs there to be one." That's a tool, not a verdict. And Chapter 4's four observations (did it feel like you afterward; did you want what it made; did it hide; who gained) let me supply the evidence before the word "parasite" arrived.

## 3. Where I skimmed

- The Chapter 2 stretch from the politician lying his country into war through the company "rooted in my need to self-aggrandize." It's the op-ed voice, and I skimmed it again.
- Chapter 7 ("The Way Through") in its second half. It's one long unheaded run of practices and claims: the last trap, the light, how I pick whom to support, partial love, niceness, the measure, awakening, catching it, looking at it, leaks, the battle. I lost the thread and started skimming for the next exercise.
- The epilogue's second half, which is mostly X's.

## 4. Confusing, out of order, repeated

- "the turning away from the light that becomes my new North Star." Still garbled.
- "If I'd looked carefully, I'd have seen my role in causing it. So I didn't—and that *was* my role in causing it." Still a knot.
- Chapter 3's "If someone had opposed any of those rooms with contempt ('you're all evil!'), that too would have fed the same thing in everyone" is orphaned. It points back three paragraphs and leads straight into the clean-sentence pairs, which belong in a practice chapter, not a diagnosis chapter. In the same paragraph the clean version is called "the healthy version" and then "the clean one."
- "Fierce is where the darkness finds it easiest to get in... it counts on our not wanting to tell them apart" (Ch. 3). The darkness acts as an agent a chapter before it's introduced. "That's the low-grade infection" uses the parasite's metaphor before the parasite.
- "That's the other big lie." What was the first?
- "The light" is defined twice: once in Chapter 2 from the second stranger, then again in Chapter 7 with the news-story exercise. The second definition is better and comes five chapters late.
- Chapter 2 says "my outer world is a reflection of that inner corruption" without the caveat (never read someone else's misfortune as their doing). The caveat only arrives in the epilogue.
- "the deepest form... contempt for the world itself" appears in the epilogue as one undeveloped paragraph, a large claim arriving out of nowhere.
- Footnotes carry content that belongs in the body: the news split, the extrapolation caveat, the Tehri Dam.
- "I don't support people primarily based on whether their policies or projects are 'good.' I try to feel whether they're turned-toward or turned-away *in general*." Out of place, and uneasy-making (see 6).
- Typos, more than I'd expect: "Betraying my conscience feel like," "it's confined to my skull" (lowercase after a period), "another person'," "with an pregnant," "I'll usually find myself usually muttering," "look,, the more," "touchy-feeling things," "as direct and consequential as mine as the oil executive."

## 5. The inner monologue

Believable, and in Chapter 1 slightly more natural than I'd have expected. "*Shit.* He's onto me. But how?" That "But how?" is exactly what the mind asks, and it sets up the mechanism. "So I push it out of mind harder." "it may be a bit underhanded, but it's for the greater good." The parasite's own voice in Chapter 6 is good: "*Look how easily I can take over even the 'good people.'*" So is "*He hurt my people; I've earned this.*" Still false: "*who can really say whether hating my enemy is good, as long as it works?*" No one calls it "hating my enemy" to themselves.

## 6. From my seat

Said better than I usually hear it:

- The parasite, handled with philosophical care. "I'll also grant it a goal, in the one sense I've earned. When I can predict a thing only by assuming it wants something, I'll say it wants it." That's Dennett's intentional stance in one sentence. It answers every worry I had about reifying the darkness, and it holds: Y never turns the habit into "a life-devouring force." "It isn't 'ego' in the everyday sense... It's the mind that counts itself as the one exception." Much closer to what Buddhists mean by self-grasping than the usual pop "ego."
- The middle way about evil: "it has no being of its own, and it must be fought. Treat it as an essence and you get contempt. Treat it as harmless and you get complicity." Each extreme gets a consequence.
- The self, handled carefully: "you hid it from *yourself*, not from some part of you. So what was it hidden from?... What you hid it from is what you call 'I' when nothing is being hidden... I'm not claiming there's a thing in there you could point to." And "I never had to produce the care. It was there until I turned from it, and the turning was all I did." That's Buddha-nature without the metaphysical furniture.
- Relativism, treated fairly: "Care gets written down in a thousand ways, which is why moral codes differ... But what differs is what people believe, and belief isn't where the knowing lives." I'd argue with it in seminar, but it's an argument, not a diagnosis of the arguer. And "Every ideology began as care, written down so it could be carried... It's that it can be followed without looking." And "a contract, a precept, a grievance procedure exist because people who were sure they had us fully in view didn't."
- Common knowledge: "If that's right, the knowledge is exactly common; if not, it's close enough for the trap to work." Careful. It doesn't claim more than the mechanism needs.
- Niceness as contempt's mirror ("said serenely while someone is still bleeding"). Still the best thing anyone has said about spiritual bypassing.

What's wrong, thin, or overclaimed:

- Sameness still slides into identity. "it's the same kind of thing that looked out of the stranger's... I mean it literally. Not that he is like me. Look for what would tell the two apart... I can't find anything." Failing to find a difference in the quality of knowing doesn't show it's one thing. Same kind, yes; one, not shown. The "two dark rooms" argument for one darkness has the same flaw: absences are individuated by where they are.
- "Goodness is one thing too. Being turned toward, seeing yourself in others, love, goodness: all one." Four things identified by fiat.
- The epilogue still has "blinding white Light... self-evidently the Source of creation, and I was That." Any Mahamudra teacher would call that an experience (nyam), not the nature, and would warn against "self-evidently." Y's new sentence, "I trusted it the way you trusted the knowledge of always having known you'd done wrong," is a better reason than X's "a million times stronger," but it doesn't answer the warning.
- "I try to feel whether they're turned-toward or turned-away *in general*" as a basis for whom to support. That worries me. It's a door to dismissing people whose politics I dislike as "turned away," which is contempt in a fresh coat, the very thing he warns about with "that's your parasite talking."
- Wetiko via Paul Levy. The footnote is respectful ("The word is theirs, and it carries more than I'm using it for"), but anchoring a Cree concept in a popular writer will bother some readers. Tehri via Eisenstein remains a weak source.
- "The part that knows whether our behavior is evil can't prove it, and the part that can prove doesn't know." Overstated. The proving part knows plenty, and the "knowing" part can be prejudice that feels like perception, as he himself shows with "I looked at him and saw it, as plainly as the color of his shirt."

## 7. Assumptions I don't share; going easy

Same as X: one Light, one faculty. I take them as a picture, which is how he marks them. Going easy on someone who hurt me? No. The note for the hurt is intact and well placed after "The Worst People": "The verdict is the one thing you don't owe them"; "Staying turned toward someone never means staying near them." But Y is missing the page that would have made me *sure* it isn't counseling passivity (see 8).

## 8. Obvious? Newly consequential?

The should-know-better check (toddler, grandparent, colleague) is here and stronger. "So even this contempt seems to carry a moral charge underneath." That joins my contempt for the "stupid" to the moral case, and I'll watch for it this week. "What I rehearse alone is what I see with afterward" is here. The catchphrases are here ("Which have you said this month?"). The clean-sentence pairs are here, but without the instruction to say something from my own week both ways, which is what made them work on me.

What Y lacks, and I noticed the gaps because they were the most usable pages I'd read: nothing on fighting institutions without the verdict (name the harm, sue, strike, organize); no Birmingham or Leipzig; no "contempt feels like fuel... Care can rest. Contempt can't afford to"; no shark-and-tornado argument; nothing about home (who gets the short answer, the child doing your face, "*Can you put the phone away while we eat?*"); no plain-words repair; no locker-room scene with the man who objects and gets turned on. Without these, Y's second half tells me how to look but not how to *act* in a fight, at a dinner table, or in front of a child. A reader who worries this book will make her soft has nothing in Y to show her otherwise except a sentence or two.

## 9. LLM-speak

Less than X, and less pasted-in feeling.

- "So we help produce the 'bad people' whose actions justify our contempt; all of us build that same division into institutions; and those institutions provide new objects against which our contempt can feel righteous."
- "So the darkness in every one of us benefits from the same thing, a darker world in which each has more cover, and faces the same way: from the same light, toward the same end... So every darkness wants the same world, faces the same way, and has nothing else to tell it from the others." The same paragraph says it twice.
- "Two seemingly-identical actions, taken from different orientations, can produce vastly different outcomes." (carried over)
- "*Every* project and agenda has this potential... countless invisible channels." (carried over)
- "It's the only way I can stop mindlessly spreading the very evil I've been so sure I'm fighting."
- "That requires profound honesty and humility—and often, help from others."
- "We have projects and agendas that are motivated by the self-serving part of us, and they create apparent good but hollow out the caring in everything they touch."
- "Being turned toward, seeing yourself in others, love, goodness: all one." Summary-slide.
- "**Caring is not knowing what to do.** The guide tells you that others matter. It doesn't tell you what to do." Three times in three sentences.
- "The part that knows whether our behavior is evil can't prove it, and the part that can prove doesn't know." Symmetry tic.
- "It's hard to describe the crushing pain of seeing this."
- "(You may be wondering about...)"

Worst stretch: Chapter 2's middle (shared with X), then Chapter 3's paragraphs on institutions and division.

## 10. Voice

His voice holds better across Y than across X. The parts I trust: plain, rueful, first person, quick to go first. "I give the finger to the driver who cuts me off, and ten minutes later I cut someone off myself and don't even register it, because I had somewhere to be." "a hot little *fuck yeah*." "I'm on the lam from the Light." "I still haven't cleaned up my act, so make of that what you will." Chapters 4 and 5 sound like a man who has thought hard and cut hard, more philosopher than confessor, but recognizably him.

Where it slips:

- Into sermon: "their only allegiance is to the light"; "There is a battle between good and evil... fought in your every thought, word, and deed"; "the wisest people are able to do in the face of far greater evils."
- Into greeting card: "It's as though the light in me is reminding the light in him that it has a partner in the fight against what has him" (Ch. 4). In Chapter 3 Y had already found a better version: "I'm telling the part of him that knows better that it isn't alone." The revision didn't reach both places.
- Into think-piece: Chapter 2's politician and company stretch.
- The epilogue's "Ideas that live in our collective shadow manifest 'externally'... lurk in the shadows of reality." That's New Thought, not him.

## 11. Flow

- Chapter 1: good. It reads cleaner than I expected, and the mechanism is tied to the thesis at once ("It only works if we both know we're the same kind of thing"). The Quiet Kind is thinner here, because its best material moved to Chapter 3, where it works better.
- Chapter 2: tighter, with a better ending ("It's as if part of my mind is working against my best interests—and against yours, too.").
- Chapter 3: a clear arc (small turns, rooms, the world, the loop), but it jolts at the orphan line and the wedged-in clean sentence. The locker room is a generic sketch now. The ecology section arrives before the reader has the picture.
- Chapter 4: the best-flowing chapter in either book. Two people, then rooms, institutions, a country, the conspiracy, then the question.
- Chapter 5: dense and right.
- Chapter 6 and the exercise: good. Pulling the exercise out was a good choice.
- Chapter 7 flows worst. It's long and unheaded, and the last trap is buried in the middle where it loses its force.
- The Picture: compact, but it leans on footnotes.
- Epilogue: the "deepest form" paragraph is dropped in.

## 12. Scores

- Recognition: 8
- Profundity: 8
- Likely to change me: 7
- Clarity: 7
- Pull: 7
- Freshness: 7
- Voice consistency: 7
- Flow: 6

In short: Y thinks better. It's the one I'd give a philosopher. But it has thrown out some of the pages I'd give everyone else.

Nadia

## nadia-xy-3-compare

Dear Leila,

Both letters are on disk, so here's the comparison you asked for.

## (a) Which is better, and why

**Y is the better book.** Not by a mile, and not everywhere, but it's the one whose argument I could defend in a seminar and whose view I could defend in a sangha.

What decided it was three chapters. Y's Chapter 4 earns the parasite. It shows me four things about my own turn (it felt like me only while it had me, I didn't want what it made, it hid, it alone gained) and only then names it. It holds the agency talk to one disciplined rule: "When I can predict a thing only by assuming it wants something, I'll say it wants it." X stages the parasite ("What does that sound like to you? / It is like a parasite") and then, four lines after calling it a habit that "doesn't need a tenant," makes it "a singular principle... a life-denying, life-devouring force."

Y's Chapter 5 delivers what X's Chapter 3 title promised and never gave. "What you hid it from is what you call 'I' when nothing is being hidden... I'm not claiming there's a thing in there you could point to." And it closes the loop the whole book opens: "The message that denies the sameness is made of the sameness, and nothing else could receive it."

And Y doesn't have X's Chapter 4. That chapter is where any philosopher or serious practitioner would put X down: the namaste error, the Snopes and blog hyperlinks, Seth, Hoffman and Kant stacked as one view, "we are literally ignoring it," the perennialist lumping of the Upanishads with the Buddhists who argued against them.

Where X won me: the second half of its Chapter 5 is the most usable writing in either book. "Against Power," "At Home," the shark, the clean sentence with an instruction to try it on my own week, repair. Its Chapter 1 Quiet Kind gave me my first real recognition, the should-know-better line, though Y moved that into its Chapter 3 and made it stronger. X's concrete scenes (the locker room, the colonization click) are more vivid.

Where X lost me: Chapter 4's errors and links; Chapter 3's reification and its dozen pasted single paragraphs; the epilogue repeating Chapters 3 and 4.

Where Y won me: the earned parasite; the careful self; the fair treatment of relativism ("belief isn't where the knowing lives"); "not a bigger act of attention, but the covering removed"; the institutional material set up in its paragraph rather than pasted in.

Where Y lost me: it threw out most of X's practical second half. Its Chapter 7 is an unheaded run. It shifts the author's findings into "you" statements. And the typos.

## (b) What Y should take from X, ranked

1. **"Against Power," whole** (X, `05.md`, middle). "Fight it. Name the harm, and the people who signed off on it. Publish the documents. Sue, strike, vote, organize, block the road if that's what it takes. None of that is the verdict." Then the verdict described exactly: "the poster that gives the CEO a demon's face, the chant about what he *is*, the joke in the group chat afterward." Then "The worker gets to feel he's defending his family from people who despise him. The regulator gets to side with the reasonable people against the fanatics." Birmingham and Leipzig ("They named Bull Connor, and nobody pretended he was a good man. What they refused to do was become what he said they were."). And the fuel paragraph: "Contempt feels like fuel because it burns hot. But it lives off something, and what it lives off is the care that brought you to the cause... Care can rest. Contempt can't afford to." Without this, Y never shows that this book doesn't make you soft. Y's "fierce, unyielding refusal... has a long record against power that needs consent" is a claim with no case under it.

2. **"At Home"** (X, `05.md`, near the end). "The contempt I swallowed at work doesn't go anywhere. It waits for someone who can't fire me." The child doing your face: "*I just did the face. That wasn't fair to him.* A child who has watched someone catch the look has learned that it can be caught." The weekly dinner: "*Can you put the phone away while we eat?* Leave off the extra. Then notice, on the drive home, whether you're rehearsing the case." Y keeps only "Something in me is constantly sensing who it's safe to treat this way."

3. **The shark check** (X, `05.md`, "The Far End"). "Do you feel contempt for a shark? A tornado? A rabid dog?... If contempt tracked how broken someone was, it would fall off at the far end, as it does for the shark. Mine rises." It's the cleanest argument in either book, and it pairs perfectly with Y's should-know-better passage.

4. **The locker-room scene as a scene** (X, `03.md`). "_She'll come around. They always do._"; "_Guys. She's actually pretty freaked out about it._"; "Within seconds, he's the one who did something wrong."; "If he'd taken the first man aside, he might have reached him. In the room, what that man knew had the others to hide behind." Y reduced it to "Ten men, gathered around. One makes an off-color joke." That loses the objector, which is the whole point.

5. **The clean-sentence instruction** (X, `05.md`). "Take something you said this week with an edge in it, and say it both ways." Y has the pairs but not the instruction, and the instruction is what made me hear my own "I assume you read the paper." Move the pairs out of Y's diagnosis chapter (Ch. 3) into the practice chapter, with the instruction.

6. **The colonization click, in the body** (X, `03.md`). "It wasn't until I saw that it's not just that they *could* use it against me, but that I *wanted* them to, that things finally clicked. The 'enemy' I was giving tools to was *the darkness itself*, which was trying to recruit me." And the room: "When I sit in a room full of people stoking such resentment, I can almost feel that pattern operating across us at once." Y squeezes this into one epilogue sentence. It's the author's most personal case and it belongs in the main line.

7. **Repair in plain words** (X, `05.md`). "*I've been looking at you a certain way for a long time, and it wasn't fair.*... The twelve-step programs keep a list for this... If I'm apologizing to feel clean, or to be seen apologizing, the extra has found another way out."

8. **Thích Nhất Hạnh's girl-first line** (X, `05.md`, "Kind, Not Nice"). "Thích Nhất Hạnh names himself as the girl before he names himself as the pirate. Read the poem without her and it's a different poem." Y has the niceness paragraph without it.

9. **The victim-blaming caveat where the mirror idea first appears** (X, `02.md`, end). "It is never my place to look at what happens to another and say (or even believe) that they must have somehow brought it upon themselves." Y's Chapter 2 says "my outer world is a reflection of that inner corruption" and leaves it there until the epilogue.

10. **The news-story exercise with the first definition of "the light"** (X, `02.md`). Y has it in Chapter 7, five chapters after it defines the light. Put it back where the word is introduced, and cut the second definition.

11. **"Every Day"** (X, `05.md`). "And they did what they do: called you brainwashed... did something in you light up: *look at that, that's exactly what they're like.* Might that have been the point?" My uncle at Thanksgiving. It caught me.

12. Smaller things worth keeping: "Those are colors I paint with; goodness is the light I paint by," with Plato's sun (X, `04.md`). "Someone could have counted the villagers. First, someone had to want them counted, and that wanting never came out of a count." (X, `06.md`). "It runs everywhere now, India included. It runs in me." (X, `06.md`). "That feeling of 'well, I could obviously never do what they do'—that's the surest sign that I still can," as a phrase the reader can catch herself saying, next to Y's better question, "which part of me needs there to be one" (X, `02.md`). The Tibetan four lines (X, `04.md`), but called what they are, the four faults that keep us from recognizing mind's nature, not "a beautiful riddle."

## (c) What Y must fix, ranked

1. **Chapter 7's structure.** It's one unheaded run: "Now the part I most want to get right," the last trap, the light again, whom I support, partial love, niceness, the measure, awakening, catching it, looking at it, leaks, the battle. Give it sections in the order the reader's questions come up, and end the practice on the last trap, as X does, not bury it mid-chapter.

2. **"You" where it should be "I."** "If you look deeply enough into the desire to make the *bad people* suffer, it's what you'll find, too." "The heat changes... You want them to *know* what they are." "You can't help but hand control to your own." "You turn away from someone because you suspect, below words..." "A deeper part of you knows..." X had these as the author's findings. They leave more room that way, and they're more believable.

3. **Sameness sliding into identity.** "it's the same kind of thing that looked out of the stranger's... I mean it literally. Not that he is like me. Look for what would tell the two apart... I can't find anything." Not finding a difference doesn't make two things one. "Same kind" is what the book needs, and the first sentence of Chapter 1 says so. And "Goodness is one thing too. Being turned toward, seeing yourself in others, love, goodness: all one." That's four identifications by fiat.

4. **Machine-written and committee passages.**
   - "So the darkness in every one of us benefits from the same thing, a darker world in which each has more cover, and faces the same way: from the same light, toward the same end... So every darkness wants the same world, faces the same way, and has nothing else to tell it from the others." Said twice in one paragraph.
   - "So we help produce the 'bad people' whose actions justify our contempt; all of us build that same division into institutions; and those institutions provide new objects against which our contempt can feel righteous."
   - "Two seemingly-identical actions, taken from different orientations, can produce vastly different outcomes." and "*Every* project and agenda has this potential... countless invisible channels." (Ch. 2, shared with X; cut the stretch to a paragraph.)
   - "We have projects and agendas that are motivated by the self-serving part of us, and they create apparent good but hollow out the caring in everything they touch."
   - "**Caring is not knowing what to do.** The guide tells you that others matter. It doesn't tell you what to do." Say it once.
   - "It's the only way I can stop mindlessly spreading the very evil I've been so sure I'm fighting." "That requires profound honesty and humility—and often, help from others." "It's hard to describe the crushing pain of seeing this." "(You may be wondering about...)"

5. **Voice lapses.** The greeting card: "It's as though the light in me is reminding the light in him that it has a partner in the fight against what has him" (Ch. 4). Use Chapter 3's own better version: "I'm telling the part of him that knows better that it isn't alone." The sermon: "their only allegiance is to the light"; "the wisest people are able to do in the face of far greater evils." The epilogue's New Thought: "Ideas that live in our collective shadow manifest 'externally'... lurk in the shadows of reality."

6. **Joins that jolt.** Chapter 3's orphan, "If someone had opposed any of those rooms with contempt ('you're all evil!')..." with the clean sentence wedged in after it. The darkness acting ("it counts on our not wanting to tell them apart") and the "low-grade infection" a chapter before the parasite is introduced. The epilogue's dropped-in "Its deepest form... contempt for the world itself." Chapter 3 opening on "Evil must be fought" with no bridge from Chapter 2's last line. "That's the other big lie," with no first one.

7. **"I try to feel whether they're turned-toward or turned-away *in general*"** as a basis for whom to support. Cut it or face it. As it stands it hands readers a polite form of the contempt the book is about.

8. **Content in footnotes**: the news split, the extrapolation caveat ("I don't expect that to count as evidence for you, and it shouldn't" is part of the honesty and belongs in the text), the Tehri Dam. And find a better source than Eisenstein for the dam.

9. **The epilogue's "self-evidently the Source of creation... I was That."** Same problem in both books. Y's new sentence ("I trusted it the way you trusted the knowledge of always having known you'd done wrong") is better ground. The fix is to let the white Light be what he saw, and stop "self-evidently" from doing the work. Y already has the right view in its body ("the covering removed"), so the epilogue should be held to it.

10. **The awareness-care argument** ("If awareness were neutral between care and harm, why would harming someone require turning away from it?... care belongs to awareness itself"). Both books bury it in the epilogue's longest paragraph, next to Darwin and "all realities." It has real lineage (Dzogchen's ground as empty, luminous, and compassionately responsive) and it's what would make the book cohere. It belongs in Chapter 5, marked as the author's view.

11. **Typos**: "Betraying my conscience feel like," "it's confined to my skull," "another person'," "with an pregnant," "I'll usually find myself usually muttering," "look,, the more," "touchy-feeling," "as direct and consequential as mine as the oil executive."

## (d) What X does better that must not be lost

The second half of X's Chapter 5. It turns a diagnosis into a life. It shows the reader how to fight institutions, how to act at a dinner table, what to do in front of a child, how to apologize, and how to tell a movement that runs on contempt from one that runs on care. Y without it is a better argument with fewer hands.

Beyond that: X's concrete scenes, especially the full locker room. X's first-person findings, which Y turned into "you." The author's own colonization story as a full passage. And X's careful line in Chapter 1's Quiet Kind, "losing respect for someone isn't the same as contempt," which Y keeps in substance in Chapter 3 ("being right about what someone can't do doesn't call for the eye-roll"). Make sure the distinction survives in words a reader can repeat.

## (e) Which reads more like one person

**Y.** It's revised throughout by one hand and has no pasted single paragraphs. X reads like an older frame with a dozen good paragraphs dropped in, each starting with a stray space, plus a Chapter 4 in a different register: popular-science explainer, reading list, links.

Best stretch of Y for voice: Chapter 4, from "Go back to the turns you've watched" through "The feeling of being the noble one fighting the evil conspiracy *is* the conspiracy." Plain, exact, unhurried. "Nothing in it says *far enough*; follow the nudge to its end and you arrive at someone who doesn't count." "The moment it puts the coordination in a people, a cabal, a face, it has become the thing it was sensing, and that misplacement has filled graves."

Worst stretch of Y: Chapter 2's politician and company passage ("Imagine a politician lying his country into war..." through "...how it's likely to affect the *whole.*"), shared with X. Close behind, the epilogue's "Ideas that live in our collective shadow manifest 'externally'."

Best stretch of X for voice: Chapter 5 from "Kind, Not Nice" through "At Home." "It waits for someone who can't fire me." "Niceness keeps the person who was harmed out of view: *it's all confusion, he couldn't help it*, said serenely while someone is still bleeding." "Care can rest. Contempt can't afford to." That's a man who knows what he's talking about.

Worst stretch of X: Chapter 4 from "Neuroscientist Anil Seth calls perception..." through the Tibetan "riddle." "Kant said much the same in 1781." "It feels like being let in on the [*great cosmic joke*](https://...)." "his answer was to [shoot back](https://www.snopes.com/...)." "The Sanskrit word *namaste* means roughly 'I bow to the light in you.'" Nobody who wrote "the look is the deed" wrote those sentences in the same mood.

So: take Y and give it X's hands.

Nadia

## nadia-xy-4-appendices

Dear Leila,

I've read the three appendices. They were the pleasant surprise of this whole exercise. If you'd handed me only the appendices, I'd have taken the author much more seriously as a thinker, and I'd have been more forgiving of the books.

## 1. What happened in me

Appendix 2 is the first time in this project that someone wrote about my own tradition and got it right. "What I've been calling the turning-away sits one step after *marigpa*. Marigpa is non-recognition: not a thing and not an act, just the nature of mind failing to recognize itself. The turning is what a self does once it's there." Most Western writers collapse those two. He doesn't. On the second stranger: "That isn't pointing-out, which introduces the nature whole, but it has the same shape." He doesn't inflate an ordinary moment into a transmission. The Samantabhadra reading is correct ("a momentary blankness, with no motive given—and the sense of self and other arises *after* it"), and so is what he draws from it: "the first turning-away doesn't need a cause, because it was never an act."

One connection was new to me, and I'll be sitting with it. Mahamudra says "the nature of thought is dharmakaya." He sets that beside his own finding that "the message that denies our sameness is made of that sameness and can be read by nothing else." The self-liberation of afflictions, mapped onto how contempt works between two people. I hadn't seen that, and I've been in this lineage fifteen years.

And this, which I want to read aloud to my sitting group: "The easiest way to misread non-fabrication is as *nothing to do*... it produces the other way of failing the book's paradox, treating the thing as harmless because it's an absence, which makes you complicit, in a form that feels like attainment. Whatever else this book is, it isn't permission." I have watched practitioners do exactly that, and I have done it.

Things that landed here and in neither book:

- From Appendix 1, the distinction between malice and contempt. Malice "need not be received (the target can be asleep while the deed is done), and where it does demand reception (a threat, a sadist's display) it needn't disavow itself as righteous. Aimed contempt needs both... So contempt holds two things in limbo where malice holds one... It's why malice is frightening and contempt is maddening." That's the best piece of conceptual analysis in the project.
- "**My self-deception isn't just how I send it. It's what I'm sending.**" and "**I am using our recognition of the same good to make him distrust it.**" Either one could open Chapter 1 better than what's there.
- "A drive wouldn't need the turn. Hunger doesn't have to look away from the bread. Harm has to look away from the person." That's the cleanest argument for evil as privation I've read outside Augustine, and more checkable than his. And "An absence can act only through the presence it's the absence of." That answers the question I kept asking in Book X: what is doing the masquerading, if evil is an absence?
- "The bank statement I won't open has the same shape on a small scale: my not opening it is steered by the very recognition I'm avoiding." That's the best example of limbo anywhere in the project, better than anything in either Chapter 1.
- From Appendix 2: "nobody builds machinery that elaborate to keep something from a part of themselves that doesn't care. The size of the hiding measures the care." And "The road starts from your worst moment rather than your best." That's the book's real contribution in one sentence.
- The epistemic honesty. "The method isn't independent: both rest on disciplined looking from the inside, so they could share a blind spot." On psychopathy, which I was about to object to, he cites the finding (the response is weak when spontaneous, present when asked for) and draws exactly the limited conclusion it supports: "it doesn't show anything in them minds. So it can't prove the care is there and merely covered." Then he offers a sadist test with three marks of "fully seeing," and calls it what it is: "a test you run on what you've seen, not one a lab can run." And the Gödel note concedes the problem I'd have raised, whether the faculty that knows is itself sound: "a problem the book carries rather than a result it has."

## 2. Which book they fit better

**Y**, clearly. Y's Chapter 4 already uses the appendix's vocabulary and its discipline: "a groove in attention," the goal "in the one sense I've earned," "It isn't 'ego'... It's the mind that counts itself as the one exception," which is Appendix 2's self-cherishing, nearly verbatim. Y's Chapter 5 carries Appendix 1's conclusion almost word for word: "Darkness can communicate only by borrowing the light as common ground... one faculty in two bodies, turned against itself. The message that denies the sameness is made of the sameness, and nothing else could receive it." Y's "awakening... not a bigger act of attention, but the covering removed" is Appendix 2's adventitious obscurations. And Y's limbo is plain "turned away," without X's conscious/aware stipulation, which fits Appendix 1's "limbo isn't the unconscious. What's in limbo is available to consciousness."

X fights the appendices in places. X's Chapter 4 says "the Buddhists get to a similar place... by rooting out self-deception," calls the four faults a "riddle," and glosses namaste as "the light in you." Appendix 2 would embarrass that chapter. X's Chapter 3 turns the darkness into "a singular principle... a life-devouring force," which Appendix 2's "denying in the same breath that it is a thing" rules out. X's "It is what some traditions call the *ego*" is exactly the muddle Appendix 2 corrects.

Where the books lean on the appendices: both point to Appendix 1 from the mechanism ("If the recursion tickles you"; "See Appendix 1 for details"). Fine. Both epilogues footnote the Samantabhadra prayer to Appendix 2. But the epilogue's "self-evidently the Source of creation, and I was That" can't lean there. Appendix 2's ground is not a creator, and nothing in it supports "Source of creation." The appendix is truer to the tradition than the epilogue that cites it.

Where they fail to lean when they should:

- Neither book gives readers any hint that the two strangers have been tested. Appendix 3 cites Braithwaite (reintegrative versus stigmatizing shaming, "with encouraging and mixed results"), and Appendix 1 cites Tangney and Dearing ("I did a bad thing" repairs, "I am bad" hides and attacks). One sentence in Chapter 1, pointing there, would answer the skeptic.
- Neither book uses Trungpa's "idiot compassion," which Appendix 2 rightly calls "this book's kind-is-not-nice under another name."
- Appendix 2 cites the Dzogchen ground's third aspect, compassionate responsiveness. That's the traditional support for the epilogue's best argument ("care belongs to awareness itself"), and neither epilogue uses it.
- Appendix 2 refers to "The line I opened the book with, from the Gospel of Philip." Neither Book X nor Book Y opens with a Gospel of Philip line. Either a version I haven't seen did, or the reference is stale.

## 3. What should move

Into the body of Y:

1. The malice/contempt distinction, condensed to a paragraph, in Chapter 1 after "Plain malice might frighten you. This maddens." Y already cut X's version of that line, so it has room.
2. "A drive wouldn't need the turn. Hunger doesn't have to look away from the bread. Harm has to look away from the person." Into Chapter 2, where evil is defined as the turn. It does in three sentences what the "What do the evil cases have in common?" section takes a page to assert.
3. The bank statement, into Chapter 1's limbo section.
4. "The size of the hiding measures the care." Into Chapter 5, near "I never had to produce the care."
5. "Whatever else this book is, it isn't permission." Into Chapter 7, near the niceness mirror, together with Trungpa's "idiot compassion."
6. One sentence pointing to Braithwaite and Tangney at the two strangers.
7. The awareness-care argument with its lineage (Dzogchen's three aspects), into Chapter 5, marked as his view. It's buried in the epilogue now.

Out of the body, into an appendix:

1. From X (if any of it survives): Chapter 4's anthology of mystics and the Seth, Hoffman and Kant paragraph. If kept at all, they belong in Appendix 2 or 3, where the author is careful. Fix the namaste gloss and the "riddle" on the way.
2. From Y: Chapter 5's common-knowledge paragraph is fine in the body, but its formal side ("If that's right, the knowledge is exactly common; if not, it's close enough") is already done better in Appendix 1's footnote. The Emperor's New Clothes, and "Unsaid is not the same as not common knowledge. What's missing, for both, is acknowledgment." Keep one, and point to the other.
3. From both epilogues: the ACIM machinery. Appendix 2's two lines on ACIM are the right weight. The epilogue spends a page on it.
4. Duplication: "Everything doing anything, the sending, the hiding, the reading, the answering, is the Light: one faculty, in two bodies, turned against itself" appears in Y's Chapter 5 and again in Appendix 1. Keep it in the body. In the appendix, refer to it.

## 4. LLM-speak, voice changes, poor flow

Appendices 1 (Section 2) and 3 are marked "written mostly by Claude." I read them knowing that, and the change of hand shows, though less than I expected.

- **Ventriloquized first person.** This is the real problem. Appendix 3 is "written mostly by Claude, an AI," then says "I've caught myself at it in earlier drafts of this very appendix" and "I only saw it because it was done to me, and I had, for once, no way of not noticing." Whose experience is that? After the disclosure, an AI claiming the author's lived experience in the first person is jarring. Either the author writes those sentences, or they go.
- Appendix 1, Section 2, is opaque in places: "Two things in the five conditions go further than the look needed. One is that the covering never touches it: the look needed the care in him to be there, obscured and not gone, not untouched." I read it three times. What five conditions? Earlier there were four points and five observations. "Obscured and not gone, not untouched" is a pile of negations.
- Committee precision: "Each is a claim about experience, open to a reader who looked and found otherwise. They aren't all of one kind: O1, O2, O3 and O5 are recalled, O4 is run when you can, and the second stranger in O5 is imagined by anyone who hasn't met one." "Of what the look needed, the first two come from the encounter alone; the rest lean on what you checked in your own case." Fine for the lawyers, but the labels (O1–O5, C, H) make it a referee report.
- Announcing: "Now the thing worth noticing, which I didn't expect when I started making this list." "The stopping places turn out to be interesting, because most of them stop at the same line." "I want to say that plainly."
- Template: Appendix 3's "**Name** got the ___. Where it stops is ___." It's readable, but by Fingarette it's a drumbeat.
- Volume: "'Thoughtlessness' is exactly right." "the strongest single predictor."
- Small things: "5. ..." as a list item in Appendix 1, which reads unfinished. "[^story] ." with a stray space. "it doesn't show anything in them minds" is grammatical ("minds" as a verb) but I tripped on it.
- Where the author's voice is plainest and best: all of Appendix 2, and Appendix 1's Section 1. "I want to name the correspondences, since it costs nothing and since pretending to have invented them would be its own small act of hiding." "If that's wrong, the mistake is mine and not the traditions'." "It surely has holes. If you find them, they're holes in my description. What I'd ask you to test is the seeing, in your own case." "I've never met the case. If you have, it counts." That's the man I trust in Book X's best pages, after ten more years of reading.

Flow: Appendix 1's Section 1 flows very well: trap, harm, what makes it contempt, the need, what the look needed, privation, belief. Section 2 is a set of separate notes, as advertised. Appendix 2 flows best. Appendix 3 is a well-ordered survey.

## 5. From my seat

What's wrong, thin, or overclaimed:

- **Levinas is missing.** Both books open Chapter 2 with the face, "until it stops being something you're looking at and becomes a place somebody is looking out of," and "From this place, try to wish them harm. For me it can't be done." That is Levinas: the face that says "thou shalt not kill." He appears nowhere, not even in "Where else to look." Sartre's *look* in *Being and Nothingness* (the look that makes me an object) is the obvious partner for "the look is the deed," and it's missing too, though Sartre appears for bad faith. Buber's I–Thou and I–It is the turning toward and away. Scheler and Nietzsche on ressentiment would sharpen the account of contempt. Simone Weil on attention sits next to Murdoch. For a philosopher, the absence of Levinas is the one omission that will look like ignorance rather than choice.
- Arendt is read a little uncharitably, against the appendix's own rule. "She doesn't ask what's *holding* it in place." In *Eichmann* and *The Life of the Mind* she does, partly: clichés and stock phrases "protect us against reality." That's a holding mechanism. The distinction he wants (hot versus cold, held versus absent) survives, but the charge is overstated.
- "Nothing tells what's looking out of you apart from what's looking out of them." Asserted flatly in Appendix 1, and it carries the step from shared nature to common knowledge. Same kind isn't one. In Mahamudra the nature is the same and the continua are distinct. Appendix 2 is careful about this. Appendix 1 isn't.
- "What I experience as myself, my awareness, and my better nature are one thing." I'd strike "myself." Y's Chapter 5 has the better phrasing: "what you call 'I' when nothing is being hidden."
- The con man paragraph in Appendix 1 says "Try to picture one doing what they do while seeing the person... I can't." That's a test of what he can imagine, not data. Appendix 2 then does it properly, with the psychopathy finding and the sadist test. Appendix 1 should point there instead of offering the thought experiment.
- Wapnick and ACIM as the "nearest thing to the fear account." Fine as a personal note. But Appendix 2 already shows that Samantabhadra plus a "fear comes second" reading does the work without ACIM. I'd let the Dzogchen account carry it.
- Paul Levy and Charles Eisenstein appear in Y's footnotes, not here. Appendix 3's research section is far stronger (Newman, Bloom and Knobe; Waytz, Young and Ginges; Harris and Fiske), and it's the right register: "None of this is evidence for the metaphysics."

What's said better than I usually hear it:

- The originality claim, made with scruple. "Most of what this book concludes is old." Then the projective-identification paragraph names exactly what's new: "Here the concealment is itself the message. What the target reads is not a feeling that's been pushed into them but the other man's relation to what he himself knows... Those five are what I'm claiming, and no more." And the shared stopping line: "most of these accounts, at the crucial moment, pick a side... Pick a side and the two-person transaction disappears." That's a real contribution to how we read Jung, Arendt, Bandura and Girard together.
- Trivers inverted: "For him the leak is a cost... The whole of my argument is that the leak is the *point*."
- Augustine handled honestly: "Augustine keeps the will and the guilt where I keep innocence and the catching of yourself. The privation is shared; the account of responsibility isn't."
- The Pali citations (AN 10.61, MN 7, MN 113, and SN 7.2's abuse "refused stays with the giver") are correct and well chosen. So is Trungpa's "idiot compassion." Someone did the reading.
- "What is not claimed" in Appendix 1. Every book like this needs that paragraph, and almost none has one.

## 6. Scores

- Profundity: 8
- Rigor: 8 (Appendix 1 Section 1 and Appendix 2: 9. Appendix 1 Section 2: 7, sound but opaque. Appendix 3: 7, fair but with gaps)
- Clarity: 7
- Voice consistency: 6 (two hands, disclosed, but the ventriloquized "I" in Appendix 3 costs it)
- Flow: 7

One last thing. These appendices make me think the author knows more than either book lets him show. The book that results from Y, with X's practical half put back and the best of these appendices moved into the body, is one I'd assign, recommend, and argue with for a long time. I mean that as the compliment it is.

Nadia

## nadia-yx-1-y

Dear Ruth,

I've finished Book Y. You asked me to read it slowly and I did, with a pencil, and I did the exercises, which I suspect you knew I'd resist and then do anyway. Here's what happened. I'll write about Book X separately and won't open it until this is sent.

**1. What happened in me.**

More than I expected. I went in braced for self-help with Buddhist borrowings and got, for the first two chapters, something closer to Sartre on bad faith written by someone who's actually been caught.

The recognition I'm carrying out of the room came from Chapter 3, and it's about the colleagues I privately think are stupid. He asks you to think of someone who can't do a thing through no fault of their own, "a toddler who can't add, a grandparent losing their words," and see if the contempt is there. It wasn't, not for my mother after her stroke. Then: "It arrives once I've decided they *should* know better; that they could, and won't. So even this contempt seems to carry a moral charge underneath. I've found them guilty of being what they are." I had always filed my contempt for slow colleagues as a matter of taste, nearly aesthetic, and therefore innocent. It isn't. I've found them guilty of coasting. I'll sit differently in Tuesday's faculty meeting, or at least I'll know what my face is doing.

Second: "**Love and contempt are addressed to the same place.** One says *I know you're in there,* the other says *there's nobody in there*, and they both say it to the somebody who is." When he asked where contempt had reached in me, I went straight to my advisor calling me "a very competent synthesizer" in front of my committee, and then to Philip presenting our framework as his. Same bruise, ten years apart. I'd never seen that they were aimed at the same place, or that it was the place love aims at.

Third, the line about the sigh. "The look on their face that tells me it landed, which I pretend not to have seen." That's me and Sam, more often than I'd like to count.

And the practical checks in Chapter 7 caught me twice. "If the harm simply stopped tomorrow, with nobody punished and nobody exposed, would you feel relief, or robbed?" Robbed. And "would I be glad if they got there before I did?" I thought of the young man in my sitting group whose practice has opened faster than mine, and found a ready-made dharma sneer ("it's just an experience, he doesn't really understand the view"). That's contempt in robes. I didn't enjoy finding it.

**2. Where I was handled.**

Mostly he asks good questions and leaves room, which I want to say first, because it's rare. But there are places he tells me what I'll find:

- "If you look deeply enough into the desire to make the *bad people* suffer, it's what you'll find, too. It is the thing we all hate; hatred itself. That's why we don't look so deeply." That closes every exit: if I don't find it, I didn't look deeply enough.
- "It would be difficult to actually do, wouldn't it? Why?" (the dehumanizing gaze).
- "Have you ever been on the receiving end of a dehumanizing gaze? Could you feel the sense in which the other person was somehow absent or turned away from themselves?" I'd have said "managed," not "absent," if he'd let me.
- Chapter 4's opening tells me four facts about my own experience ("You didn't want what it made." "It hid itself.") after three chapters of asking.
- "Can you feel that?" (the corpse).
- "when I really stop to taste what it's 'made of,' this becomes impossible to ignore."
- "I think anyone who watches their own contempt closely for long enough will find it too." That's in the Picture, which he says has no check, so it's doubly odd.
- "None of it stays inside you, and part of you already knows that."
- "We all know the distinction. We just don't trust that we know it."

And one structural habit: whenever a claim needs support, "part of me knows," "some part of them senses," "they feel it, whether I register this or not." Once or twice that's a finding. By Chapter 3 it's the book's universal solvent, and I started hearing it as a way of not having to argue.

**3. Where I skimmed.**

The middle of Chapter 2, lines about the politician, the supporter, the cause and the company (the paragraph beginning "*Every* project and agenda has this potential"). Four examples of one point. The bus-seat detour in Chapter 2 that ends "It's a subtle issue, and we won't get into all the nuances here." The ecology paragraphs in Chapter 3. And the second half of the Epilogue, from "Ideas that live in our collective shadow manifest 'externally'" on, which reads like journal notes.

**4. Confusing, out of order, repeated.**

- "And what I've been calling *darkness* is exactly that self-deception: the turning away from the light that becomes my new North Star." I read it three times. Grammatically the light becomes the North Star, which is the opposite of what he means.
- "If I'd looked carefully, I'd have seen my role in causing it. So I didn't—and that *was* my role in causing it." Too neat; I had to supply the sense.
- "We feel compelled to hate them because we can *see* that they know what they're doing. And they do. But they also don't. And we can see this—but also we don't." The know/don't-know refrain starts to sound like a trick: when a contradiction arises, both horns are declared true.
- The same exercise twice: Chapter 2 ("Think of a wrong that was especially hard to admit. Was it hard because you knew less about it...?") and Chapter 6 ("Think of the smallest wrong you've had to admit to yourself, and the biggest. Which took longer?"). Same conclusion.
- The "letter of the law / spirit" argument about defining goodness appears in Chapter 2 and again, nearly word for word, in Chapter 7. The cynic's "who's to say?" appears three times.
- The contempt-for-the-slow point is in Chapter 1 and again in Chapter 3. The Chapter 3 version, with the toddler check, is the one that matters.
- Three checks for the same thing: the vengeance button (Ch 1), "disappointed if they got better" (Ch 7), "relief or robbed" (Ch 7). The last is the best; three is a lot.
- The factory farm appears in Chapters 4 and 6.
- "Life," "what's doing the looking," "care," "the light," "awareness," "better nature," "love," "goodness," "Light," "Love," "Source," "Home," "That." By Chapter 5 I had a list in the margin and couldn't tell which claim was about which.

**5. The inner monologue.**

Yes, mostly, and it's the best thing in the book. "He's **bad**, dammit." "*Shit.* He's onto me. But how?" "It's the *good* kind of darkness—the kind that all good people must harbor, to fight the real kind!" That's how a mind sounds when it's lying to itself and half-enjoying it. "*I'm really worried about Lisa*" is perfect; I have said that sentence. The executive's "The counter-evidence just feels a little more convincing" is exactly how motivated reasoning feels from inside. One slip: "*I mean, who can really say whether hating my enemy is good, as long as it works?*" Nobody says "as long as it works" to themselves. That one's a strawman.

**6. From my seat.**

What's said better than I usually hear it:

- Chapter 7's instruction to "look at the contempt itself, not the person. Where is it? What's it made of?" That's the instruction my first Kagyu teacher gave me, nearly to the word: look at the klesha, not its object. I did it with the Philip contempt and it thinned, as he says. "The tightening is worth learning, because it usually comes before the story does, and it doesn't argue." Correct and beautifully put.
- "Pity, which passes for compassion and is contempt with a soft face... Equanimity can do it too, when it's a way of filing someone under *deluded*... So can practice itself: the sigh at my own mind on the cushion." Those are Buddhaghosa's near enemies, said about the speaker rather than preached at a room. I sigh at my own mind every morning.
- "Niceness keeps the person who was harmed out of view: *it's all confusion, he couldn't help it*, said serenely while someone is still bleeding." Every sangha I've belonged to needed this paragraph. It's Trungpa's idiot compassion without the jargon.
- "*Goodness means serving life. This man isn't serving it. Therefore, hating him is good.* ... It's the orientation turned into an idea." This is what my teachers say about the view becoming a position, applied to ethics. And "Every ideology began as care, written down so it could be carried." Lovely.
- Chapter 4's deflation of the parasite: "a groove in attention so old that its shape looks like intent... That's all a habit is, and it doesn't need a tenant." That's vasana, habit-energy, with no homunculus. And "When I can predict a thing only by assuming it wants something, I'll say it wants it" is Dennett's intentional stance in one sentence. I relaxed reading it.
- "When I'm sure someone was fully aware of what they were doing, I've made them a villain. When I'm sure they had no idea, I've made them a machine." That's the whole responsibility debate in two sentences.

What's wrong, thin or overclaimed:

- **The inference from "nothing tells them apart" to "one."** Chapter 5: "Look for what would tell the two apart... what's doing the looking. I can't find anything." Then: "one faculty in two bodies." Not finding a distinguishing mark isn't finding identity. In Madhyamaka, when you search for the self in me and in you and find nothing, you've found emptiness in both, not one shared looker. That slide, from emptiness into a monism, is precisely what the Tibetan commentators spent centuries warning against. He also says "the same kind of thing" in one sentence and "I mean it literally. Not that he is like me" in the next. Those are two different claims (same kind versus numerically one) and he slides between them. The Picture runs the same faulty step on the dark side ("the dark in one is the same as the dark in the other"): two dark rooms lack light in the same way; they aren't one darkness. The One Light and the One Devil rest on the same invalid move.
- **The argument from grammar.** "Look at the grammar: you hid it from *yourself*, not from some part of you. So what was it hidden from?" The reflexive pronoun settles nothing; "I hid it from myself" is the sentence that generates the paradox of self-deception, not one that solves it. He softens it right after, which helps, but it's still load-bearing.
- **"Life" for awareness.** "When I look most deeply into what I am, I find life... what's doing the looking." What's doing the looking is awareness, knowing. "Life" is biological, and it lets him extend the claim to trees and arches, but that's a pun doing metaphysical work.
- **The Epilogue's vision.** "Blinding white Light; infinite Love. It was self-evidently the Source of creation, and I was That." I don't doubt he had it. But in my tradition, experiences of brilliant light, bliss and boundlessness are nyam: experiences arising in mind, to be neither rejected nor clung to, and specifically not to be taken for the nature of mind. "Self-evidently the Source of creation" is the eternalist reading Madhyamaka exists to refute, and "I was That" is Advaita, not Mahamudra. Then he sets *A Course in Miracles* beside the Aspiration of Samantabhadra. The Dzogchen prayer does describe ignorance arising from not recognizing one's own display, and his "a lapse, not a crime, and not a real departure either, since there's nowhere else to be" is genuinely close to it. But ACIM's "primal guilt" and "betrayed God" have no counterpart there; in Dzogchen there's no guilt in the arising of ignorance at all. He's merging two very different stories.
- "Then Darwin is just the way we explain Love in our particular reality. One way or another, Love shows up in all realities, because they're ultimately made of it." That's where he lost me.
- "part of me always saw that I was doing it, and that part was essentially God, and God is reality, so knowledge of my wrongdoing always takes physical shape in my 'external' world." Three identity claims chained with "and... and... so." As testimony, fine. As reasoning, nothing.
