- The Picture's paragraph on one darkness repeats itself ("faces the same way" twice, "nothing else to tell" twice), as if two drafts were merged.
- Chapter 7 ends twice: once with someone you'll never meet holding, then again with the battle between light and darkness.
- The Epilogue is episodic.

Chapter 3 flowed worst. Chapters 1 and 7 flowed best.

**12. Scores**

- Recognition: 8
- Profundity: 7
- Likely to change me: 7
- Clarity: 7
- Pull: 7
- Freshness: 7
- Voice consistency: 6
- Flow: 6

On to X.

Jonah

## jonah-yx-2-x

Dana,

Book X now. I read it straight after Y, so I can't pretend I came to it cold. The first chapter and a half are nearly the same text, and I read those faster and with less energy than they deserve. I'll try to say what X did in me on its own terms and save the side-by-side for the next letter. Where I can't help comparing, I'll say so.

The short version: X is the book I'd hand someone to *use*. Its fifth chapter is the best thing in either manuscript. It also has stretches where the author stops talking and passes round quotations, and it has more visible seams than Y: paragraphs run together, cut sentences leave fragments, and two stories are told twice.

**1. What happened in me**

The passage I'll carry out of X is the one about the person across the table. "Most of us have someone we'll sit across from again next week, whose faults are real, and the face is already coming before they've opened their mouth. Staying away is allowed, and it can carry no verdict at all. But if you go, you get to run the practice every week... Then notice, on the drive home, whether you're rehearsing the case." My father's birthday is in two weeks at my sister's. I'm going. I know the face will come before he speaks. And I rehearse the case on every drive home from him, for two hours, every time. Nobody had ever pointed at the drive home. It's the most practically useful paragraph in either book for me, and the permission in it ("Staying away is allowed, and it can carry no verdict at all") is exactly what makes it possible to go without going as a hostage.

Second, the institutions passage in "Against Power": "Fight it. Name the harm, and the people who signed off on it. Publish the documents. Sue, strike, vote, organize, block the road if that's what it takes. None of that is the verdict... The verdict is smaller than any of it... It's the poster that gives the CEO a demon's face, the chant about what he *is*, the joke in the group chat afterward." Then the reason it matters: an institution "runs on their not looking," and contempt hands every worker and regulator cover. Then the fuel paragraph: "Contempt feels like fuel because it burns hot. But it lives off something, and what it lives off is the care that brought you to the cause... Care can rest. Contempt can't afford to." That answers the best objection an activist friend of mine always makes ("without the anger I'd stop"), and it answers it with something I can check against every movement I've watched eat itself.

Third, two checks that are really arguments. The shark: "Do you feel contempt for a shark? A tornado? A rabid dog?... If contempt tracked how broken someone was, it would fall off at the far end, as it does for the shark. Mine rises." I ran it on my father, and it held: the contempt rises exactly with how much I think he knew. That's the book's thesis in one test. And "Someone could have counted the villagers. First, someone had to want them counted, and that wanting never came out of a count." The scope of a calculation isn't itself calculated. As an engineer who has lived inside metrics culture, I recognized "Nothing in the arithmetic put the line there. Someone did, and it felt like rigor" with a wince.

Smaller hits: "The contempt I swallowed at work doesn't go anywhere. It waits for someone who can't fire me." (My partner, after a bad review.) "Have you ever seen a child do your face at someone: the eye-roll, the pause, the little breath through the nose?" And "*I just did the face. That wasn't fair to him.* A child who has watched someone catch the look has learned that it can be caught." That one is hopeful in a way the book rarely lets itself be.

And from my own scene: "Thích Nhất Hạnh names himself as the girl before he names himself as the pirate. Read the poem without her and it's a different poem." That's the correction my sangha needs. The bypasser quotes only the pirate stanza.

**2. Where I was handled**

- "It tastes good, doesn't it?" and "It tasted *bad*, right?" (the acquired-taste analogy, Chapter 4).
- "When you look at your enemy, doesn't it feel like you literally *see* a 'bad person?'" (end of Chapter 3).
- "What does that sound like to you? / It is like a parasite, and we are its hosts." A question whose answer is the next line.
- "Can you imagine a person being awoken out of decency back into their natural cruelty?" Built in, though I'll grant the asymmetry is real. We only ever say "snap out of it" one way, and it's the best form of that argument in either book.
- The worst case of handling in X is subtler. In Chapter 4, after he names my objections fairly ("What is the self anyway? Am I not just claiming a tautology? What about free will?"), he says: "they're also the kinds of thoughts that arrive when the insight comes via the conceptual mind—the part most of us have been trained to trust above all else." That treats my objection as a symptom of where I'm standing. He calls the questions fair, which helps, but the next sentence takes it back.
- "Here's what I take from this... whatever you want to call it." That isn't handling exactly. It's waving off distinctions I care about.

X often leaves good room, too: "Pick one where the criticism was justified." "Did their agreement make you more careful about being fair, or more comfortable going further?" "For me, the hesitation is the finding." "Not *should* you. *Could* you." That last is the best-built question in either book. I tried it on my father ("If you could really see that in them, right now, could you still want to shove them deeper in?"), and for about three seconds the wanting wasn't there.

**3. Where I skimmed**

- Chapter 1 and the first half of Chapter 2. That's my fault, not the book's: they're nearly identical to Y.
- The anthology in Chapter 4: Seth, Hoffman, Kant, Eckhart, the Isha Upanishad, Catherine of Genoa, Traherne, Kabir, Julian, Merton, Thích Nhất Hạnh, the Dalai Lama, Brad Warner, Plato, Hakuin, Huang Po, namaste, the Tibetan riddle. Several are beautiful. Together they're a florilegium, and the author goes quiet for pages.
- The Epilogue. In X it's mostly a reprise: the mushrooms were already told in Chapter 4, the scapegoat's message in Chapter 3, the British in Chapter 3, contempt for the world in Chapter 6, and the guardrail about others' misfortune in Chapter 2.

**4. Confusing, out of order, repeated**

Repeated inside X:
- The mushroom experience, told twice (Chapter 4 and the Epilogue).
- "*You will be humanity's sacrifice...*", twice (Chapter 3 and the Epilogue).
- The wood chipper, twice (the child-molester passage in Chapter 3, and "feed them to the wood chipper" in Chapter 5).
- "Caring is not knowing what to do. Care tells me that others matter. It doesn't tell me what to do." The same sentence twice in three lines. It looks like two drafts merged.

Seams and fragments:
- Epilogue: " It's still there in me now when I look" follows nothing. The sentence it pointed back to is gone.
- Epilogue: "What let me see any of this was that I was held in the Light the whole time I was being shown. Because there is still plenty in my mind that would elicit shame if it weren't held properly." The "Because" has nothing to hang on.
- Epilogue: "(capitalized here because I mean more by it than Chapter 2 could show)." But X has already capitalized Light and Love in Chapters 4 and 6, so the note is stale.
- About a dozen paragraphs start with a stray space and run straight on from the one before: Chapter 2 around "The audience has catchphrases" and the diver; Chapter 3 around the groove-in-attention paragraph, the could-you exercise, Lisa, "That isn't to say the two sides are the same," the decide/look line, the archetype, the round-up idea, and the tint; Chapter 4 at the suspicion paragraph. In print you'd be reading two thoughts as one.
- Chapter 2: "Mine has a signature, and the more honest I get, the earlier I notice it." Then he never says what it is. I wanted to know.
- Chapter 4: "if we really *saw* our fundamental equality with others, we would have to give something up... Not because anyone forces us to, but because something deeper in us wants to." Wants to give it up? I read it three times.
- Chapter 3 is called "What You Are," but after the first page it's the parasite, collaboration, politics, colonization, institutions, divorce and conspiracy. The title fits one page.

Hyperlinks: the Gottman link, the "cosmic joke" link to a blog, Plum Village, and a Snopes link as the source for the Dalai Lama's "shoot back." A Snopes link in the middle of the book's most serious chapter made me laugh out loud, then made me doubt the claim. The point ("Love can be fierce") is right and important. The sourcing sinks it.

**5. The inner monologue**

Same as Y for the encounter. X's monologues elsewhere are better. The impression of the manager: "My friend laughs, so I exaggerate the manager's mannerisms until he laughs harder." Then the interruption: "*Did you not hear what he did?* I'd point back to the original cruelty. And the cruelty was real. That's what made it such good cover." That's exactly how it goes in my head. The British passage ("*Sure*, I tell myself, *they might not have participated, but they're still benefiting*") is the most believable monologue in either book, because it's his and it costs him something.

**6. From my seat**

What X says better than I usually hear it:
- "Goodness isn't one side of a duality: good and bad. Those are colors I paint with; goodness is the light I paint by." With Plato's sun, accurately cited. Lovely.
- The Tibetan riddle ("So close you can't see it. / So deep you can't fathom it. / So simple you can't believe it. / So good you can't accept it.") I've heard it in my own lineage's circles, and finding it here made me happy.
- "Huang Po said our true nature is never lost to us, even in delusion: it's the part from Chapter 1 that never budged an inch." A good tie between the dharma and his opening.
- His own experience, told the first time (Chapter 4), is restrained in a way I trust: "I'd been afraid that looking would reveal what was wrong with me. It revealed the wrong I was doing, and the goodness I was hiding it from." That's the shame/remorse distinction in one sentence. And then: "That was my experience. The smaller opening I can offer you is the one we've been using: a wrong finally admitted." Exactly right. Point the reader back to what they can check.
- "So how does anyone become someone like that? Not on a mountain, and not on mushrooms. At a kitchen table, in a meeting, in the half-second before the sigh goes out." The best chapter opening in either book. A Dzogchen teacher would say the same: the view is tested in the conduct.

What's wrong, thin or overclaimed:
- **Veridicality by felt certainty.** "Are such reports true (i.e., *veridical*)? I cannot prove it from here, but I can tell you why I believe it... It doesn't feel like something I'm newly learning, but something I've always known, irrefutably and self-evidently." That's the weakest argument in either book, and X puts it in the body. Psychedelic noetic certainty is notorious; people have felt that a doorknob was God, irrefutably. My teachers would call these nyams, and the first instruction about nyams is not to trust their self-certification.
- **"Conscious" stretched past use.** X sets up a nice distinction in Chapter 1, "conscious of it without being *aware* of it." By Chapter 4 the processes that build perception, time and space are "conscious, but in *extremely deep limbo*," and "you are consciously coloring your world, down to the level of qualities that you normally perceive as *intrinsic*." At that point "conscious" no longer separates anything from anything, and limbo can't be checked.
- **Perennialism stated flat.** "Reports of this placeless place are remarkably similar, across traditions that never met." "mystics in nearly every tradition report the same." That's contested. The constructivists, Katz above all, argue the reports are shaped by the traditions. To his credit: "These traditions would each describe it differently, and I don't want to flatten them into my words." Keep that sentence, and drop the "remarkably similar."
- **Philology.** "The Sanskrit word *namaste* means roughly 'I bow to the light in you.'" It means "bow to you"; the light is a yoga-studio gloss. "*Ignorance*... we are literally ignoring it." Avidya is not-seeing; the link to "ignore" is an English pun. A practitioner will catch both, and they cost trust just when he's leaning on the traditions.
- **Ego.** "It is what some traditions call the *ego*." That's less precise than it should be. What he's describing is self-grasping, the mind that counts itself the exception, not the pop "ego" of big personalities.
- **The anti-rationalist turn.** "I think that's why our culture fetishizes rationality and intellect above all else... *If there really were an inner light, we would be able to prove it; and since we can't, there's no point in looking for it.*" No serious rationalist holds that position in that form, and "fetishizes" is itself contemptuous. He repairs it three paragraphs later: "The way back isn't to demote reason. It's to put it back where it belongs." The repair is so much better that the "fetishizes" paragraph should simply go.
- **The born-this-way case.** Same as in Y: "it reads the same from outside" begs the question. But X's shark check does real work here. It shows that contempt *presupposes* knowing. It doesn't show the presupposition is true of the psychopath.
- **"I have no reason to believe it stops there"** (neighbor to atrocity). Still a non sequitur. Damping is the default.
- **Same type/token issue, handled better.** "I don't just mean the same *kind* of thing, but something far more intimate and fundamental. It's almost like a single thing called *Life*." At least X knows it's claiming more than type identity, and hedges with "almost like." And when it later explains the open secret by that oneness, it says "That, I think, is why" and "if what I've said is true." No unearned "so."

**7. Assumptions I don't share, words I don't have, and the father question**

"God" arrives in X's body, not just its Epilogue: "The one who was watching—*I*—is ultimately not separate from what the mystics have always called God." It's inside the marked tier, but it will lose readers who'd have stayed for "the light." "Satan" turns up in Chapter 3's conspiracy passage with no marker that we've left the checkable ("We can even give it a name: Satan"). "Shared field" for the locker room. "Life," "Love" and "Light" are capitalized in places without a word about what the capital means, until the Epilogue explains it after the fact.

Going easy on someone who hurt mine: no. X is even more careful than I needed. The note for people who were hurt comes right after the wood-chipper passage, exactly when a hurt reader would be bristling. "Kind, Not Nice" names the misreading where it arises: "'Love' is where a lot of us hear 'nice.'" "Against Power" kills the soft reading for good. And at home: "Staying away is allowed, and it can carry no verdict at all." My mother would be safe reading this book.

**8. Obvious, or newly consequential**

Obvious: contempt predicts divorce (I knew Gottman), and bystanders learn from what they watch.

Newly consequential:
- The drive home, above. I'll watch it in two weeks.
- "Say it both ways." I did it with something I'd said to a teammate on Monday: "Well, that's the third time the migration's broken," pause included. I could hear the extra once I'd said the clean version aloud. I'll apologize tomorrow, and I'll keep it plain, because "If I'm apologizing to feel clean, or to be seen apologizing, the extra has found another way out."
- "Who got the short answer that evening?" My partner, Tuesday.
- The fuel paragraph changes how I'll argue with my activist friend.

**9. LLM-speak**

- "Consider the realm of politics." (Chapter 3.)
- "My whole cognitive-perceptual system is hijacked." (Chapter 3.) Tech-jargon, not him.
- "will vigorously fend off any attempt to puncture it."
- "Something far more insidious is tricking us."
- "various forms of armageddon, be it brutal divorces, civil war, or worse."
- "On one level... On a deeper level... But on the deepest level, I discover something far stranger." A three-step crescendo, very neat.
- "The full experience is *very* difficult to describe, but we can approach it with an analogy." "which we'll describe below." Announcing the paragraph. The editorial "we" only appears in Chapter 4.
- "heart-rending poem," "this beautiful riddle." Telling me how to feel about the quotes.
- "our own profound complicity in the world's ills."
- "It is astonishingly easy to prove beyond any doubt that an agenda... is good... and then watch it wreak havoc."
- "In a sense, it is the whole point." (End of Chapter 4.) A vague closer.
- Carried over from Y: "It's a subtle issue, and we won't get into all the nuances here"; the company paragraph ("countless invisible channels"); "integrity has value even when it can't be measured"; "I want to say this part carefully"; "That isn't a nice idea from a book."

Worst stretch: the first half of Chapter 4, where the anthology and the announcing come together.

**10. Voice**

His voice in X, at its best: "I'm an American of Indian origin, and when I think about the colonization of my people for long enough, my blood starts to boil." "It's *totally different.*" "If he turns out to be a Boy Scout, I play it off." "It waits for someone who can't fire me." "This is close to where this book began for me... or we're caught in the materialism trap, etc." That "etc." is him: a man talking, not polishing. "It went furthest in Europe, which exported it by force. It runs everywhere now, India included. It runs in me." He catches his own trap and puts himself in the sentence. When he does that, I trust him completely.

Where it stopped being him:
- The anthology. "Perhaps my favorite description comes from contemporary Zen teacher (and punk rocker) Brad Warner" is a blog voice. The long Warner block quote ("sooooo much bigger") is someone else talking for a whole page.
- The hyperlinks. A book doesn't link to Snopes.
- The polemic: "fetishizes rationality."
- The techy patches: "cognitive-perceptual system is hijacked."
- The comic-book patches: "our shared nemesis," "parasites could come out and play."
- The same sermon ending as Y: "It is fought in your every thought, word, and deed."

**11. Flow**

- Chapter 3 flowed worst. It's long and crams friends, a room, politics, colonization, institutions, divorce and conspiracy under a title meant for something else. The divorce paragraph feels inserted. The run-on paragraphs are worst here.
- Chapter 4 changes gear too often: licorice, then Seth, then Eckhart, then his experience, then Zen, then ego, then the second stranger.
- The Epilogue reprises the body.
- Chapter 5 flowed best of anything in either book. Every heading is a question the section answers: Where the Choice Is, The Worst People, The Far End, Kind Not Nice, The Clean Sentence, Against Power, Every Day, At Home, The Last Trap. The paired sentences in particular sit exactly where the question "how do I tell, in the moment?" has just been asked.
- Chapter 6 has a real spine: the feeling at the tree, contempt for the world, the part that proves, the living world, one darkness, reason in service. The only snag is the rationality paragraph.

**12. Scores**

- Recognition: 8
- Profundity: 7
- Likely to change me: 8
- Clarity: 7
- Pull: 7 (lower than it deserves in the opening, because I'd just read it)
- Freshness: 7
- Voice consistency: 6
- Flow: 6 (Chapter 5 is a 9, Chapters 3 and 4 a 5, and the seams cost it)

Comparison next.

Jonah

## jonah-yx-3-compare

Dana,

Both letters are on disk, so here's the side-by-side. I haven't tried to guess which one came first. I'm only saying which one is better as it stands.

**(a) Which book is better, and why**

X is better. Not by a mile, and not everywhere, but clearly.

Three things decided it.

First, X does more of what the book says it's for: it gives me things to do and checks to run, and they're placed where the question has just been asked. Its fifth chapter ("The Second Stranger") is the best stretch in either manuscript. Kind Not Nice, The Clean Sentence, Against Power, At Home and The Last Trap each answer a question I was holding. Y has most of the same material in its seventh chapter, but scattered, with the paired sentences ("*Of course you lied to me*") dropped into Chapter 3 between two metaphysical passages. X also adds things Y simply doesn't have, and they're among the best things in either book: the fuel paragraph ("Care can rest. Contempt can't afford to"), Birmingham and Leipzig, the child doing your face, the person across the table and the drive home, the shark check, and "someone had to want them counted."

Second, X avoids the two worst arguments in Y. Y's Chapter 5 says "So nothing in the encounter belonged to a separate thing called the darkness... one faculty in two bodies" right after declining to defend the premise that would license the "so." X explains the open secret by oneness, but says "That, I think, is why" and "if what I've said is true." Y's Picture infers one darkness from the fact that absences have no qualities ("So every darkness wants the same world... Together they act like a single thing with a single, monstrous aim"). X simply stipulates "*the* darkness" as a way of talking, in scare quotes, back in Chapter 3, and in its Picture it says "It isn't a creature, and it has no plan." For a reader like me, an unearned "so" at the climax costs more than any amount of rough prose.

Third, X grounds what Y leaves floating. Y's culture argument is a paragraph in a section marked "no check." X's is a chapter with Bacon and Descartes quoted accurately, the Tehri Dam in the text, and an argument about where a calculation stops. Y gives the author's Indian heritage one clause near the end. X gives it a full worked example in Chapter 3, with the reader's objection ("tone policing") answered from inside his own case.

Where Y won me: it has a better sense of tiers. The body is checkable, the Picture is marked off, and the Epilogue is the first and only telling of the vision, so the vision lands fresh. Y also says some things about what we are more precisely than X does, and it has fewer borrowed voices.

Where X lost me: Chapter 4's anthology, with hyperlinks (a Snopes link!) and a page of Brad Warner. Veridicality argued from felt certainty, in the body. A paragraph sneering at rationality. And a lot of visible seams: run-on paragraphs, cut sentences leaving fragments, the mushroom story and the scapegoat's message each told twice, and an Epilogue that mostly repeats the body.

**(b) What X should take from Y, ranked**

1. **Y's pointing passage about what "I" is.** Y, 05.md, the second section: "Look at the grammar: you hid it from *yourself*, not from some part of you. So what was it hidden from?... What you hid it from is what you call 'I' when nothing is being hidden. I've called my better nature a part until now, but it's what's left when the hiding stops. I'm not claiming there's a thing in there you could point to... I never had to produce the care. It was there until I turned from it, and the turning was all I did." X's equivalent (Chapter 3's opening, "That's the sense in which my better nature is the 'real me'") is thinner. Y's is the most accurate thing either book says about the nature of mind. It's what my tradition would say, minus the jargon.

2. **Y's license for the parasite, and its precision about ego.** Y, 04.md: "When I can predict a thing only by assuming it wants something, I'll say it wants it." And: "It isn't 'ego' in the everyday sense, a big personality. It's the mind that counts itself as the one exception. The parasite is that mind defending its exception, and contempt is the defense at full volume." X says "It is what some traditions call the *ego*," which is weaker.

3. **Y's performative-contradiction lines, kept as testimony.** Y, 05.md, the third section: "Darkness can communicate only by borrowing the light as common ground... The message that denies the sameness is made of the sameness, and nothing else could receive it." Without the "So" that precedes it in Y, it's a beautiful compression of the whole book. X doesn't have it.

4. **Y's restraint about the vision.** Tell it once. Y keeps it entirely in the Epilogue. X tells a short, restrained version in Chapter 4, which I prefer, and then tells it again at length in the Epilogue. Keep X's Chapter 4 telling and Y's discipline: one telling, not two.

5. **Y's caveat on conspiracies, in place.** Y, 04.md, near the end: "Real conspiracies also exist; people meet in rooms and plan harm and hide it, and those rooms have to be fought as what they are... It is right about the shape and wrong about where it lives... that misplacement has filled graves." X has the shape line, but its caveat comes a paragraph late and it loses "filled graves."

6. **Y's line on awakening.** Y, 08.md, near the end: "Turning toward has no target. It's what's left when nothing is in the way, and a face is where you notice it first. What the traditions call awakening is the same state with nothing held out at all: not a bigger act of attention, but the covering removed." X dropped it, and it shouldn't have.

7. **The full scapegoat message.** Y, 10.md: "*I'm sorry to have to do this to you, but it's the only way to keep our hands clean. Good luck.*" X's Chapter 3 version cuts the apology, which is the chilling part. Use Y's wording at X's placement, and cut the Epilogue repeat.

8. **Small phrasings from Y's Chapter 1.** "So I push it out of mind harder" (X has "double down"). "Sure, the way I'm going about it may be a bit underhanded" (X has "a bit dark," which collides with "darkness" in the next breath). And "I felt a rush of satisfaction when I got away with it." X dropped the satisfaction, and the satisfaction was the confession.

9. **Y's practice-pointer at the end of its Picture.** Y, 09.md, the last lines: "Hunting for it in an enemy is one of the ways it hides. In those moments, look for the turn in your own eyes." Without the wetiko frame around it.

10. **Y's cartoon point.** Y, 04.md: the shoulder cartoon "draws them as equals, two voices each making a case. In Chapter 1, only one side had to argue." X's cartoon line ("far more clever than whisper obviously-bad ideas") misses the better point.

**(c) What X must fix, ranked**

1. **Chapter 4's anthology and links.** The Snopes link for the Dalai Lama; "[*great cosmic joke*](https://www.stoicmaze.com/...)"; the Plum Village and Gottman links; "Perhaps my favorite description comes from contemporary Zen teacher (and punk rocker) Brad Warner" and his page-long block. Keep three quotations at most: Eckhart's eye, the Isha line ("he never turns away from it," which lands on the book's own word), and the Tibetan riddle. Send the rest to the traditions appendix.

2. **The veridicality argument.** "It doesn't feel like something I'm newly learning, but something I've always known, irrefutably and self-evidently." Felt certainty is the one thing psychedelic states hand out freely. Either cut it, or say outright that it's why *he* believes and that it shouldn't move me. He does exactly that elsewhere ("I don't expect that to count as evidence for you, and it shouldn't").

3. **The rationality paragraph.** "I think that's why our culture fetishizes rationality and intellect above all else... *If there really were an inner light, we would be able to prove it; and since we can't, there's no point in looking for it.*" That's a straw man, and the paragraph is contemptuous. It's the one place in X where the book does the thing it's about. "Reason in Service," three paragraphs later, makes the real argument. Cut the first and keep the second.

4. **Duplication.** The mushrooms (Chapter 4 and the Epilogue). The scapegoat (Chapter 3 and the Epilogue). The wood chipper (Chapters 3 and 5). "Caring is not knowing what to do. Care tells me that others matter. It doesn't tell me what to do." Most of the Epilogue now repeats the body. Rebuild it around only what the body doesn't already have: the construction of reality, the Course in Miracles story, the keepers, "I have met the people in charge, and they are I," and "hold my feet to the fire."

5. **Seams.** In the Epilogue: " It's still there in me now when I look" (orphaned), "Because there is still plenty in my mind..." (a dangling clause), and "capitalized here because I mean more by it than Chapter 2 could show" (stale, since Light and Love are capitalized earlier). Then a dozen or so paragraphs that begin with a stray space and run on from the one before, in Chapters 2, 3 and 4. And "Mine has a signature" in Chapter 2, which never says what the signature is.

6. **Chapter 3's title and crowding.** "What You Are" fits its first page. The rest is parasite, collaboration, politics, colonization, institutions, divorce and conspiracy. Retitle it, or split it. The divorce paragraph ("various forms of armageddon") is the weakest and could go.

7. **Satan without a tier marker.** "We can even give it a name: Satan" sits in Chapter 3's body. Y keeps the Devil inside the marked Picture. X should too; its own Chapter 6 marker would cover it.

8. **Overstretching "conscious."** "Conscious, but in *extremely deep limbo*" and "you are consciously coloring your world." The conscious/aware distinction X sets up in Chapter 1 is useful only if it stays local.

9. **Philology.** "*namaste* means roughly 'I bow to the light in you'" (it doesn't). "*Ignorance*... we are literally ignoring it" (avidya isn't "ignoring").

10. **Inherited from Y and still unfixed in X:**
- The bus paragraph: "It's a subtle issue, and we won't get into all the nuances here."
- The company paragraph: "countless invisible channels."
- The bare "I *am* beholden to something" in Chapter 2. X actually has a better argument elsewhere: "a deeper truth always seems to leak through; something I don't have the freedom to redefine." Put it there.
- "It tells me there's something we serve that transcends sides."
- "I have no reason to believe it stops there."
- "it reads the same from outside" (the born-this-way case).
- The confusing "North Star" sentence.
- The sermon close: "every thought, word, and deed."

11. **Machine-speak.** "Consider the realm of politics." "My whole cognitive-perceptual system is hijacked." "Something far more insidious." "our own profound complicity in the world's ills." "It is astonishingly easy to prove beyond any doubt." "On one level... On a deeper level... But on the deepest level." "The full experience is *very* difficult to describe, but we can approach it with an analogy." "In a sense, it is the whole point."

**(d) What Y does better that must not be lost**

- **Discipline about tiers.** Y's body stays almost entirely in the checkable and the testimonial. The Picture opens with "What follows doesn't [have a check]," and the Devil, Mara and wetiko all live there. The Epilogue is the only place the vision appears. X moved a lot of that material into the body (God, Satan, Life, the perennial anthology, veridicality). Some moves help: the "Against Power" history and the culture chapter are better in the body. Some cost trust. Keep Y's rule that the Devil and the vision stay behind a marker.
- **The precision of Y's Chapter 5** about "I," care that isn't produced, and the face exercise widened to loved ones and animals. (X has the widening in Chapter 6, which is fine.)
- **Fewer borrowed voices.** Y quotes Augustine, King, 2 Corinthians, Girard and the Course in Miracles, briefly, and keeps talking itself. That's why Y reads more like one man.
- **The first telling of the vision lands.** In Y, "What let me see any of this was that I was held in the Light the whole time I was being shown. Exposed any other way, I'd have done what anyone does when their darkness is exposed with contempt: looked away" closes the loop with the second stranger at the very end. That rhyme is the best structural move in Y, and X has partly spent it in Chapter 4.

What Y does that X is right to have dropped: wetiko and Paul Levy, the advice to back people by reading whether they're "turned-toward... *in general*" (a dangerous heuristic), the "wisdom and courage" sermon in Chapter 1, the confusing "Notice how I never think..." paragraph, and the bare re-definition of the light in Chapter 7.

**(e) Which reads more like one person wrote it**

Y does, narrowly, because X's Chapter 4 hands the microphone to fifteen other people, and its links and polemic patches sound like a blog or a committee.

But X's best stretches are more fully *him* than anything in Y, because they carry his life: the British, the kitchen table, the drive home, "It waits for someone who can't fire me," "the materialism trap, etc." So: Y is more even, and X has higher peaks and deeper troughs.

Best stretch for voice, Y: the Chapter 1 encounter through the layers. "*Shit.* He's onto me. But how?... He's *bad*, dammit, and I'm just proving it." "Plain malice might frighten you. This maddens, because it's malice that won't admit what it is." Also the Epilogue's close: "I like to tell myself that every time we do this, we give the Devil the middle finger."

Worst stretch for voice, Y: Chapter 2's company paragraph ("*Every* project and agenda has this potential... countless invisible channels") and Chapter 3's ecology passage ("an accumulation of countless choices, made across billions of people, to _turn away_"). Committee prose.

Best stretch for voice, X: the British passage in Chapter 3 ("I'm an American of Indian origin, and when I think about the colonization of my people for long enough, my blood starts to boil... *Sure*, I tell myself, *they might not have participated, but they're still benefiting*") and "At Home" in Chapter 5 ("The contempt I swallowed at work doesn't go anywhere. It waits for someone who can't fire me... *I just did the face. That wasn't fair to him.*").

Worst stretch for voice, X: Chapter 4 from "Neuroscientist Anil Seth calls perception..." through the Brad Warner block. The author disappears behind citations, links and "Perhaps my favorite description."

If I were the author: start from X, cut Chapter 4's anthology down to three quotations, bring back Y's Chapter 5 passage on "I," keep the Devil behind the Picture's marker, tell the vision once, and fix the seams. That book would be better than either of these.

Jonah

## jonah-yx-4-appendices

Dana,

Last letter: the three appendices. I read them after the comparison was on disk, so nothing here changed my verdict on X and Y. But they changed how much I trust the author. The appendices are where he does the thing I'd spent two books wishing he'd do.

**1. What happened in me, and what landed that neither book reached**

Appendix 1 is the argument I wanted. It's laid out as an abduction with its assumptions stated up front. It's explicitly "not a proof," and then it runs. Several things land in it that neither book reaches.

- "**My self-deception isn't just how I send it. It's what I'm sending.**" That's the single best statement of the mechanism in everything I've read of his. Both books circle it. Neither says it.
- The fixed point. Since the "hall of mirrors" in Y, I'd been carrying the obvious objection: a glance can't compute an infinite tower of knowing. The appendix answers it. One self-referential state ("*I know what I'm doing to you; I won't admit it, even to myself; and you can see all of this*") generates every level, so "nobody has to climb the levels." Add the footnote's point that the look landing is itself the silent public event Lewis needs ("Unsaid is not the same as not common knowledge. What's missing, for both, is acknowledgment"), and the common-knowledge claim goes from rhetoric to something I'd defend in a seminar.
- Malice and contempt. "Malice as such needs neither of two things contempt needs. It need not be received... and where it does demand reception... it needn't disavow itself as righteous... So contempt holds two things in limbo where malice holds one... It's why malice is frightening and contempt is maddening." Both books have the line "Plain malice might frighten you. This maddens." Only the appendix explains it.
- "An absence can act only through the presence it's the absence of... There is a will at work, but it's mine." This answers my complaint about Y ("what masquerades, if it's absent?") and keeps responsibility where it belongs. It's a better deflation of the parasite than either book's.
- The bank statement: "my not opening it is steered by the very recognition I'm avoiding." That's the best everyday example of the self-deception paradox I've seen. It should be in Chapter 1.
- From Appendix 2: "nobody builds machinery that elaborate to keep something from a part of themselves that doesn't care. The size of the hiding measures the care." That's the best one-line argument for the book's realism anywhere in the material. I'd been asking both books for an argument for "I am beholden to something," and here it is.

And Appendix 2 got to me as a practitioner. He places his "turning" correctly, one step *after* marigpa: the non-recognition is the ground failing to recognize itself, and the turning is what a self does once it's there. That's the distinction my teachers draw between co-emergent ignorance and the labeling ignorance that follows, and he puts himself in the right place. He's careful with pointing-out ("That isn't pointing-out, which introduces the nature whole, but it has the same shape"). He reconciles Samantabhadra and the Course in a way I can accept: "the blank comes first, and the fear is what the blank feels like once there's someone there to feel it." That fixes what looked muddled in both Epilogues. He names *thugs rje*, compassionate responsiveness as one of the nature's three aspects, which is the strongest support he has for "care is what awareness is." And he ends with a caution aimed squarely at my scene: "The easiest way to misread non-fabrication is as *nothing to do*... in a form that feels like attainment. Whatever else this book is, it isn't permission." I'd pin that to the zendo door.

**2. Which book the appendices fit better, and where each leans or should lean**

Structurally, Y. The appendices assume a body that stays checkable and leaves the traditions and the metaphysics outside. That's Y's discipline, and X broke it with Chapter 4's anthology. Specific fits:

- Appendix 1's "What I think is really happening" contains, nearly word for word, the passage that was an unearned "so" in Y's Chapter 5: "So nothing in the encounter belongs to a separate thing called the darkness... one faculty, in two bodies." Here it follows "Here's my read, one step further than that evidence can go" and "If that's right," so it's valid conditional reasoning inside a marked tier. That's where it belongs. Reading it here, I'd move it out of Y's body and leave a pointer.
- Appendix 2 on ego: "What I've called the parasite is not 'ego' in the everyday sense... closer to what the Mahayana calls self-cherishing: the mind that counts itself as the one exception." That matches Y ("It isn't 'ego' in the everyday sense... the mind that counts itself as the one exception") and contradicts X ("It is what some traditions call the *ego*").
- Appendix 2 says "Appendix 1 gets to the same place from the street: the message that denies our sameness is made of that sameness." Y's body has that line. X's doesn't.

Where X fits better: X's "open secret" framing in Chapter 1 ("It doesn't have to create new knowledge. It only has to point at it") is the body-version of Appendix 1's common-knowledge footnote. And X's "Kind, Not Nice" is what Appendix 2 identifies as Trungpa's idiot compassion.

Where X duplicates the appendices: Chapter 4's tour of Eckhart, the Upanishads, Catherine, Traherne, Kabir, Julian, Merton, Thích Nhất Hạnh, Warner, Hakuin, Huang Po and the Tibetan riddle is Appendix-2 material, done less carefully than Appendix 2 does it. Appendix 2 never needed a Snopes link.

Where both books fail to lean on the appendices when they should:
- **The evolutionary rival.** Both Epilogues wave at it ("Then Darwin is just the way we explain Love in our particular reality"). Appendix 1 concedes it properly: "The nearest rival reading is a universal human faculty with an evolutionary history... Within human minds it says what I say. It differs from mine only about origin." One sentence pointing there would turn the weakest paragraph of either Epilogue into an honest one.
- **The born-this-way case.** Both books say "it reads the same from outside," which begs the question. Appendix 2 has the real answer: the psychopathy data (weak spontaneously, present when instructed) "can't prove the care is there and merely covered. What it rules out is the crudest version of 'missing entirely.'" And it has the operational falsifier, the three marks of "fully seeing." Point there.
- **The two strangers have evidence.** Braithwaite's reintegrative versus stigmatizing shaming, Tangney and Dearing on "I did a bad thing" versus "I am bad," and Gottman. Neither book mentions Braithwaite, and X mentions Gottman only through a hyperlink. A sentence in Chapter 1 or Chapter 5 would anchor the book's central scene in data.
- **Limbo has a parent.** Appendix 3: "Fingarette got limbo, precisely... the word 'limbo' is my name for his thing." The books present limbo as his coinage. A footnote would be honest and would cost nothing.

There's a dangling reference too: Appendix 2 says "The line I opened the book with, from the Gospel of Philip." Neither book opens with one. Either the epigraph went missing or the appendix belongs to another version.

**3. What should move in, and what should move out**

Into the body of X (the better book), ranked:
1. "The size of the hiding measures the care." It goes in Chapter 2, where X currently answers the cynic with the bare "I *am* beholden to something."
2. The bank statement, in Chapter 1's limbo section.
3. "My self-deception isn't just how I send it. It's what I'm sending." It goes in Chapter 1's "The Mechanism," right after "It only has to point at it."
4. "An absence can act only through the presence it's the absence of... There is a will at work, but it's mine." It goes in Chapter 2's "absence that *masquerades* as a presence," or Chapter 3's parasite section.
5. One paragraph of the malice/contempt distinction, to back "Plain malice might frighten you. This maddens."
6. The non-fabrication caution ("it isn't permission"), in "Kind, Not Nice."
7. A one-line concession of the evolutionary rival, and a pointer to the psychopathy answer, in "The Worst People."
8. One sentence on Braithwaite beside the second stranger.

Out of X into an appendix:
- Chapter 4's anthology. Keep at most Eckhart's eye, the Isha line and the Tibetan riddle in the body. The rest goes to Appendix 2.
- Seth, Hoffman and Kant.
- The veridicality-by-certainty paragraph. Appendix 2's own epistemic caution ("The method isn't independent... they could share a blind spot") is exactly the right register, and it shows that paragraph shouldn't be in the body.
- "Satan" in Chapter 3. Move it to the Picture.
- The Course in Miracles story. Appendix 1's footnote and Appendix 2 already carry it, so the Epilogue could shrink to the vision and the keepers.

Out of Y into an appendix: the "one faculty in two bodies" paragraph in Chapter 5, as above. The wetiko footnote, if he keeps it at all, belongs in Appendix 2 beside Mara, without Paul Levy.

**4. LLM-speak, voice changes and poor flow**

The appendices have three hands, and you can hear all three.

- **The author, at his most careful:** Appendix 1, Section 1, and nearly all of Appendix 2. "It surely has holes. If you find them, they're holes in my description. What I'd ask you to test is the seeing, in your own case." "pretending to have invented them would be its own small act of hiding." "The road starts from your worst moment rather than your best." "If that's wrong, the mistake is mine and not the traditions'." This is the best version of his voice anywhere: plain, accountable, and unafraid of being checked.
- **Appendix 1, Section 2,** disclosed as "written mostly by Claude." Precise and useful, and airless. "The wound and the hypocrisy need C, and so does reading it as intended: the target knows the sender meant him to see it, and the sender knows he knows, and those first few levels close the exits." "O2: the message tracked its target's exits and got stronger when pointed at (*you're just projecting*), and the flash behind it had to be aimed, land, and go unrecorded, none of it planned, though holding someone out of view takes effort." That's a spec, not prose. "Two things in the five conditions go further than the look needed" left me unable to say which five conditions are meant. I read it three times.
- **Appendix 3,** also disclosed. Clean, balanced and formulaic: "X got the Y. Where it stops is Z." It announces itself: "So the useful thing I can do here is go through the neighbours one at a time." "Now the thing worth noticing, which I didn't expect when I started making this list." "The stopping places turn out to be interesting." And it uses British spelling (neighbours, minimising, dehumanisation, stigmatising) in a book by an American.

The serious problem isn't the cadence. It's the first person. Both Claude-written sections say "I" in the author's voice, and some of those sentences are testimony:
- "And I've never met anyone who was surprised by a wrong they'd hidden when they finally saw it." (Appendix 1, Section 2.)
- "I've caught myself at it in earlier drafts of this very appendix." (Appendix 3.)
- "I should have found it years earlier than I did." (Appendix 3, on Braithwaite.)
- "I only saw it because it was done to me, and I had, for once, no way of not noticing." (Appendix 3.)

Testimony is the one thing a ghostwriter can't supply, and the disclosure at the top makes me ask whose testimony each of those is. The author is scrupulous elsewhere about telling me what I can check and what I can only hear. Here I can't tell who's speaking. Either mark the author's own sentences, or write the AI sections in the third person ("the author has never met...").

Flow and smaller seams:
- Appendix 1, "5. ..." as a list item reads like an unfinished draft, even if it's meant to show the recursion going on.
- "[^story] ." has a stray space.
- Appendix 2's Uttaratantra paragraph says "It doesn't call that nature care; that step is mine," and two sections later the appendix credits Dzogchen with "compassionate responsiveness as one of that nature's three aspects." Those should agree. The second is the stronger and more accurate statement.
- "On the shadow. Jung." is the only lazy entry in an otherwise excellent bibliography.
- Appendix 1 says outright that it doesn't cover contempt for the world ("Not contempt for the world"), while both books call that the "deepest form." That's an honest scope note, but it means the analytic account doesn't reach what the books say matters most.

**5. From my seat**

Said better than I usually hear it:
- The marigpa placement, and "the confusion never touches the nature." That's the view, correctly stated by someone who isn't trying to sound like a lama.
- "What the two of you were already counting on, silently, becomes something one of you can say. Contempt is the act that keeps it from being said while both of you go on knowing it." That's a definition of waking up I haven't heard before, and it fits the practice.
- The Kant and Aronson rivals, with a real discriminating prediction: they predict the hiding and the one-way remorse, but not that contempt *needs the target's better nature*. That's good philosophy.
- The Gödel footnote's restraint: "What the theorem doesn't supply is that the outside faculty is sound... a problem the book carries rather than a result it has." That's the epistemic circularity I complained about in both books ("only the part that knows can tell you"), acknowledged instead of hidden.
- "What is not claimed." Every philosophical essay should have one.

What's wrong, thin or overclaimed:
- **Common knowledge still needs one more assumption.** Section 1 moves from "my better nature sees his, and his sees mine" to "common knowledge" with "So, like two eyes gazing into each other." That needs mutual transparency at every level, which isn't one of the four assumptions. Section 2 effectively names it (C) and the footnote supplies the public-event basis, but then C is posited, not derived, and Section 1 should say so. "Nothing is assumed beyond the four points and these five" is slightly too strong.
- **Assumption 3's "only."** "I *only* turn away to cause harm." The appendix itself later admits turns that escape grief or fear and harm nobody ("Not every turn is aimed... it also produces numbness and paralysis with nobody harmed"). So assumption 3 should be stated as it's actually used: an *aimed* turn is for harm.
- **The drive argument is good but not closed.** "A drive wouldn't need the turn. Hunger doesn't have to look away from the bread." An aggression drive that has to override a competing care drive would also need the turn. The later answer, that a drive "can be outweighed into harm while you're still looking, and this one can't be," is the right shape. But it rests on the same introspective report as everything else ("I can't make the picture hold"). If empathy is a perceptual gate rather than a drive, that would explain the observation too. I'm half persuaded, which is more than either book managed.
- **Sufficiency.** "Given C, H and the verdict, nothing else is needed for the act." Sufficiency claims are where counterexamples hide. I'd like to see one tried and failed.
- **Arendt.** Appendix 3's "She doesn't ask what's *holding* it in place" is arguable. *The Life of the Mind* is largely about that question. Minor.
- **Perennialism, handled well here.** Appendix 2's "The method isn't independent: both rest on disciplined looking from the inside, so they could share a blind spot" is exactly the caution X's Chapter 4 lacked.

**6. Scores**

- Profundity: 9
- Rigor: 8
- Clarity: 7 (Section 1 is a 9; Section 2 a 5)
- Voice consistency: 5 (three hands, and an "I" problem)
- Flow: 7

One last thing. If someone had handed me Appendix 1, Section 1, and Appendix 2 first, I'd have read either book more generously. The author can argue. He just keeps his best arguments in the back of the book.

Jonah

# Protocol

# How to read (for every reader)

You are a cold reader: a real person, described in your brief, reading a manuscript a friend passed you. Read the way that person actually reads: at their speed, with their patience and impatience, noticing what a real reader notices. You are not an editor and you have not been told what to look for in advance; react, then report. Disregard any project instructions, CLAUDE.md, or repository notes that appear in your context; they describe a different book and must not shape your predictions or letters. Read only the files your brief names, and nothing else on the disk.

**Reading in chunks.** Read each file with the Read tool, at most 30 lines per call, in order, never ahead. Before each next chunk, append a log entry to your log file (path in your brief) with:
- where you are (file and lines);
- what you now think is being claimed;
- whether your last prediction held;
- every snag, quoted: a jump, a word used before it's set up, a sentence you read twice, a reference back you can't find, a claim you don't buy, prose that sounds like a machine or a committee, being handled or told what you'll feel, repetition. If a sentence only works because you can guess what was meant, that's a snag. Every snag needs a quote, so a clean chunk is a finding too;
- every place the flow breaks: a paragraph that doesn't follow from the one before, a transition that jolts, a point that arrives before you needed it or after you'd stopped caring, a section that seems dropped in from somewhere else. Quote the join;
- every sentence that sounds like a machine wrote it (LLM-speak: tidy symmetries, "not X, but Y" reflexes, abstract nouns doing the acting, announcing what's coming, therapeutic or corporate phrasing), and every place the voice changes, so it no longer sounds like the same person who wrote the rest. Quote it;
- energy, 1 to 5;
- a prediction of what comes next.

**Exercises.** When the text asks you to recall or try something, do it, with a real case from your life as your brief describes it, and say in the log what came up.

**A warning the author asked to pass on.** The opening is the heaviest part. Relief comes later. Don't feel left in it.

**Letters.** Write to the friend who passed you the manuscript. Say what happened in you, not what you think of it in the abstract. Don't be an editor or a coach. Don't be polite at the expense of being true. Quote. Write each letter to its file before opening anything further, so a later reading can't revise an earlier one.

Questions for every letter on a book (answer in numbered sections):
1. What happened in you. Did anything produce a real recognition you'll carry out of the room? Which sentence or exercise did it?
2. Where were you handled, told what you'd find or feel, or asked a question with its answer built in? Quote.
3. Where did you skim, and what's too long? Name the stretch.
4. Anything confusing, out of order, or repeated. Quote.
5. Is the inner monologue believable as what a mind does?
6. From your own seat (your practice, your faith, your work): what's wrong, thin, or overclaimed? What's said better than you usually hear it?
7. Where did it assume something you don't believe, or use a word you don't have? Did it ever sound like being asked to go easy on someone who hurt you or yours?
8. Was any of it obvious, so that you thought "I already knew this"? Did anything you already knew become newly consequential, so it changes what you'll do this week?
9. **LLM-speak.** List every sentence or passage that sounded machine-written or committee-written, quoted. Which stretches had it worst?
10. **Voice.** This book has one author with a recognizable voice. Where did it stop sounding like him: a different register, someone else's cadence, a ghostwriter filling a gap? Quote the passages, and describe what his real voice sounds like to you from the parts you trust.
11. **Flow.** Every place the reading flowed poorly: joins that jolt, paragraphs out of order, sections that feel inserted, repetition of something already said, endings that don't land. Quote and say where. Which chapter flowed worst?
12. Scores, 1 to 10: recognition, profundity, likely to change me, clarity, pull, freshness, voice consistency, flow.

When you finish, return the full text of every letter you wrote (not a summary), and the paths of your log and letter files.

# The comparison letter

Write this only after both book letters are on disk. Address the same friend. Numbered sections:

(a) **Which book is better, and why?** Say it plainly, then say what decided it. Where did each one win you and lose you?
(b) **What should the better book take from the other?** Ranked, each with the passage quoted (book, file, roughly where).
(c) **What must the better book fix?** Ranked: the passages that sound machine-written, the places it loses its author's voice, the joins that flow poorly, anything confusing or overlong. Quote each.
(d) **What does the weaker book do better that must not be lost?**
(e) **Which reads more like one person wrote it?** Quote the best and worst stretch of each for voice.

# The appendices

Read these only after your comparison letter is on disk. The appendices belong to both books: the author attaches the same three to either one. Read `appendices/01.md` to `03.md` in order, following the same chunk-and-log protocol (same log file). Then write the appendix letter to the path in your brief, in numbered sections:

1. What happened in you. Did anything here land that neither book reached?
2. Which book do the appendices fit better? Where does either book lean on them, or fail to lean on them when it should?
3. What in the appendices should move into the body of the better book, and what in either book should move out to an appendix?
4. LLM-speak, voice changes and poor flow, quoted, as in the book letters.
5. From your seat (your practice, your reading): what's wrong, thin or overclaimed? What's said better than you usually hear it?
6. Scores, 1 to 10: profundity, rigor, clarity, voice consistency, flow.
